On the relation between the subadditivity cone and the quantum entropy cone
Temple He, Veronika E. Hubeny, Massimiliano Rota

Abstract
Given a multipartite quantum system, what are the possible ways to impose mutual independence among some subsystems, and the presence of correlations among others, such that there exists a quantum state which satisfies these demands? This question and the related notion of a pattern of marginal independence (PMI) were introduced in Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv , and then argued in Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst to be central in the derivation of the holographic entropy cone. Here we continue the general information theoretic analysis of the PMIs allowed by strong subadditivity (SSA) initiated in Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv . We show how the computation of these PMIs simplifies when SSA is replaced by a weaker constraint, dubbed Klein’s condition (KC), which follows from the necessary condition for the saturation of subadditivity (SA). Formulating KC in the language of partially ordered sets, we show that the set of PMIs compatible with KC forms a lattice, and we investigate several of its structural properties. One of our main results is the identification of a specific lower dimensional face of the SA cone that contains on its boundary all the extreme rays (beyond Bell pairs) that can possibly be realized by quantum states. We verify that for four or more parties, KC is strictly weaker than SSA, but nonetheless the PMIs compatible with SSA can easily be derived from the KC-compatible ones. For the special case of 1-dimensional PMIs, we conjecture that KC and SSA are in fact equivalent. To make the presentation self-contained, we review the key ingredients from lattice theory as needed.



1
Introduction

An important endeavor in quantum information theory is the derivation of universal constraints satisfied by information quantities that characterize various properties of quantum states. For some information quantities, the classification of these constraints has been completed, in the sense that it has been possible to show that no additional independent constraints can exist besides the known ones. For example, this is the case for the quantum relative entropy, as it was shown in
Ibinson_2006
that it cannot satisfy inequalities other than
non-negativity
(also known as
Klein’s inequality
) and
monotonicity
(also known as the
data processing inequality
).

Interestingly, this program is much more complicated in the case of the von Neumann entropy, and only two fundamental inequalities are currently known, namely
subadditivity
(SA) and
strong subadditivity
(SSA), which may be understood respectively as non-negativity and monotonicity of
mutual information
, a measure of the total amount of correlation between subsystems. Since both these inequalities are implied by the monotonicity of relative entropy, one may wonder if these could be the only inequalities. The fact that this cannot quite be the case is clarified by the existence of
constrained
inequalities
Linden:2004ebt
;
Cadney_2012
, i.e., inequalities which are satisfied only when other inequalities are saturated. However, it remains an open question whether additional unconstrained inequalities exist. The eventual discovery of new inequalities would likely have important implications, since the saturation of an entropy inequality is typically associated with a particular structure of the density matrix with interesting properties. For example, the saturation of subadditivity is related to independence between subsystems, while the saturation of strong subadditivity is attained only when the density matrix is a quantum Markov chain
Hayden_2004
.

While these investigations often aim at the analysis of constraints which are universal, in the sense that they hold for arbitrary quantum states, a similar program can also be pursued for restricted classes of states, like stabilizer states or classical probability distributions. For classical probabilities, the von Neumann entropy reduces to the Shannon entropy, which additionally satisfies
monotonicity
and, for four or more parties, infinitely many new inequalities
Zhang1998OnCO
;
Makarychev:2003
;
Dougherty:2006
;
Matus2006
. Similarly, the entropy of stabilizer states has been shown to satisfy all “balanced” classical inequalities
Gross_2013
and a certain type of
linear rank inequalities

LindenRuskaiWinter
.

A class of particular interest for us is that of
geometric states
in quantum gravity, in the context of gauge/gravity duality
Maldacena:1997re
;
Gubser:1998bc
;
Witten:1998qj
. More precisely, by a geometric state we mean a state of a holographic conformal field theory which describes a classical bulk geometry. In this context, the von Neumann entropy can be computed using the HRRT prescription
Ryu:2006bv
;
Hubeny:2007xt
, and has likewise
been shown to satisfy new inequalities specific to this class. The first of such
holographic entropy inequalities
, known as
monogamy of mutual information
, was found in
Hayden:2011ag
, while a systematic search for new inequalities was started in
Bao:2015bfa
. In particular, it was shown in
hayden2016holographic
that this class of states satisfies all stabilizer entropy inequalities.

Drawing on the ideas of
Hubeny:2018trv
;
Hubeny:2018ijt
, some of the authors argued in the recent work
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
that all holographic entropy inequalities can be derived from the solution of a conceptually simpler problem, dubbed the
holographic marginal independence problem
(HMIP). This is the holographic version of the general
quantum marginal independence problem
(QMIP) introduced in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
for arbitrary quantum states. Intuitively, the QMIP asks the following question: For a given multipartite quantum system, if we demand that certain collections of subsystems are independent while
all
others manifest some correlation, is there a density matrix whose marginals satisfy these demands? An arbitrary choice of such demands was defined in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
as a “pattern of marginal independence” (PMI), so the QMIP asks which PMIs can be realized by arbitrary quantum states.

As mentioned above, the independence among subsystems is captured by the vanishing of mutual information, or equivalently by the saturation of SA. Since for a multipartite quantum system there exist multiple linearly dependent instances of SA, many combinations are trivially not realizable by either linear dependence or SA. By taking into account these basic restrictions, one can then focus on a more meaningful set of possibilities. Following
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
, we will then update the original definition of a PMI from
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
to ignore all the possible demands which are incompatible with linear dependence and SA. As we will review, a convenient way to formalize the QMIP with this notion of a PMI is via the construction of polyhedral cone in the space of entropy vectors,
1
1

1

As we will review in detail, an entropy vector is a vector whose components are the entropies of all subsystems of a system described by a given density matrix.
carved by all instances of SA, which will be called the
subadditivity cone
(SAC). The PMIs can then be defined as the linear subspaces spanned by the faces of the SAC.

For any number of parties, the SAC is an outer bound to the quantum entropy cone, and one can easily imagine that most of these PMIs are non-realizable in quantum mechanics. This is indeed the case, and at least for a small number of parties, the key reason is SSA. Specifically, for most faces of the SAC, all vectors in their interior violate SSA and therefore are not entropy vectors for any density matrix. The goal of the present work is to continue the general analysis initiated in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
for arbitrary quantum states, explore in more detail the structure of the set of PMIs which are allowed by SSA, and suggest a new route for the explicit computation of this set.

From a quantum information theoretical perspective, the analysis presented here is a necessary step, following
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
, towards the solution to the QMIP. This in turn could lead to new information about the structure of the full quantum entropy cone, as well as an important set of restrictions on the form of possible new entropy inequalities. For the class of geometric states, the hope is that this would allow us to answer a deep question about the very origin of the holographic constraints on the entropy. The results of
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
indeed strongly suggest that the essence of these constraints is captured by the set of holographically realized extreme rays of the SAC. Quantum mechanics imposes severe constraints on the possible extreme PMIs, but does the holographic set-up impose even more stringent restrictions? If it does not, then combined with the result of
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
, we would have a complete characterization of the holographic entropy constraints. Otherwise, there must exist even more fundamental constraints whose origin remains elusive.

To explicitly construct the set of PMIs which are allowed by SSA, one way to proceed would be to slice the SAC with all instances of SSA, and then determine the PMIs of the faces of the resulting cone
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
. This procedure however is highly inefficient, since many faces would correspond to the same PMIs. Therefore, as we will explain in detail, to characterize the set of PMIs which are allowed by SSA, we will consider a different constraint, which is weaker than SSA but has the advantage of being formulated solely in terms of SA. This constraint on the form of a PMI, which we will call
Klein’s condition
(KC), arises from the fact that the factorization of a density matrix is not only a sufficient condition for the saturation of SA, but also a necessary one.

Physically, KC states that any subsystems of independent systems remain independent. Since this is a constraint solely on the form of a collection of SA inequalities, we can impose it on the set of PMIs without having to introduce new entropy inequalities. More specifically, KC will be phrased in terms of
down-sets
in a certain partially ordered set of instances of mutual information, and it can be thought of as a
combinatorial
constraint on the
lattice of faces
of the SAC. This formulation will allow us to show that the set of PMIs compatible with KC has the structure of a lattice, and by constructing this lattice explicitly for
{\sf N}\leq 4
, we prove a series of results about the structure of this lattice for an arbitrary number of parties. The usefulness of this formulation will be most evident in the proof of a theorem that identifies, for any number of parties, a specific face of the SAC which contains all the extreme rays that can possibly be realized by quantum states. Since this face is lower dimensional, this result dramatically simplifies the explicit computation of these extreme rays
He:2022wip
.

This analysis will also show that KC and SSA are not equivalent constraints for the QMIP, and that KC can only be seen as an approximation to SSA for the QMIP.
2
2

2

While it is obvious that KC is strictly weaker than SSA for vectors of entropy space, this result is non-trivial for PMIs, since the compatibility of a PMI with SSA only requires the existence of a single SSA-satisfying vector on the corresponding face of the SAC.
Indeed, for
{\sf N}\geq 4
, we will prove the existence of KC-compatible PMIs corresponding to faces of the SAC that violate SSA in the interior and therefore are not realized by quantum states. Nevertheless, we will show that the set of PMIs allowed by SSA is also a lattice, with the same meet operation as the lattice of KC-compatible PMIs, and we will comment on how this property allows for the construction of the former lattice from the latter. Furthermore, by taking advantage of the computational speedup obtained from
5
, we will be able to construct all extreme rays of the SAC that are KC-compatible for
{\sf N}\leq 5
and verify that they all satisfy SSA. This observation will motivate us to conjecture that for an arbitrary number of parties, the approximation of SSA by KC is in fact exact for 1-dimensional PMIs.

The plan of the paper is as follows. In §
2
we first review the precise definition of a PMI along with the notions of realizability and compatibility with SSA, and then we introduce KC and the poset of instances of the mutual information. In §
3
we look at the structure of the set of PMIs satisfying KC, showing that it is a lattice, deriving some of its properties (for an arbitrary number of parties), and explaining their implications for the QMIP. In §
4
we comment on realizability, the structure of the set of realizable PMIs for general classes of quantum states, and the relation between KC and SSA. We end in §
5
with an extensive discussion of open questions and interesting future directions of investigation.

2
Patterns of marginal independence and Klein’s condition

We begin by reviewing in §
2.1
the basic definitions of entropy vectors, entropy space, and entropy cones. We next introduce in §
2.2
the mutual information arrangement (MIA), the subadditivity cone (SAC), and the pattern of marginal independence (PMI). The notion of a PMI was originally introduced in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
, but we will instead use the updated, more “geometric” definition from
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
. We will discuss the realizability of PMIs in §
2.3
, where we give a precise definition for a PMI to be compatible with strong subadditivity (SSA), and also introduce KC as a necessary condition for such compatibility. Finally, in §
2.4
, we introduce the poset of instances of mutual information, and show how KC can naturally be phrased in terms of its down-sets.

2.1
Preliminaries: entropy vectors and the quantum entropy cone

Consider an
{\sf N}
-party quantum system, where we denote the parties numerically as
1,2,\ldots,{\sf N}
.
3
3

3

Throughout this paper, for small
{\sf N}
we will also oftentimes denote the parties alphabetically as
A,B,C,\ldots
.
Given a density matrix
\rho
corresponding to a state in this system, we can associate to it a vector
\vec{\sf S}(\rho)
whose components are the subsystem entropies
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}
, where
\mathscr{I}\subseteq[{\sf N}]\equiv\{1,2\ldots,{\sf N}\}
.
4
4

4

We will always implicitly restrict to
\mathscr{I}\neq\varnothing
.
These vectors live in the vector space
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
, with
\sf{D}=2^{{\sf N}}-1
, referred to as
entropy space
.

While every density matrix corresponds to a vector in entropy space, not every vector in entropy space is the vector of entropies of a density matrix. We will refer to any vector in entropy space as an
entropy vector
, even if it is not the vector of entropies for any density matrix, and say that it is
realizable
if such a density matrix exists. A trivial illustration of the fact that not all entropy vectors are realizable is that any entropy vector that is not in the positive orthant of entropy space is not realizable, since entropies are constrained to be non-negative, i.e.,
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}\geq 0
for all
\mathscr{I}
.

It was shown in
1193790
that, for an arbitrary number of parties
{\sf N}
,
the set of realizable entropy vectors is a convex cone in entropy space, called the
{\sf N}
-party
quantum entropy cone
(QEC
N
).
5
5

5

More precisely, it is the topological closure of the set of realizable entropy vectors which is a convex cone
1193790
.
The QEC
N
is known for
{\sf N}=2,3
, and it coincides with the polyhedral cone specified by all instances of the following inequalities:

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}\geq{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{K}}\qquad

subadditivity (SA)

(1)

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{K}}\geq{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}\qquad

Araki-Lieb (AL)

(2)

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{J}\mathscr{K}}\geq{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{J}\mathscr{K}}

strong subadditivity (SSA)

(3)

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{J}\mathscr{K}}\geq{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{J}}

weak monotonicity (WM)

(4)

where we use the shorthand notation
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\mathscr{K}}
for
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\cup\mathscr{K}}
, and we always assume that
\mathscr{I}\cap\mathscr{K}=\varnothing
(and similarly for other pairs of indices).
As we already mentioned in the introduction, for larger
{\sf N}
no other unconstrained inequality is known.
6
6

6

The reason why we have not included the non-negativity of the entropy in the list above is that it is not a fundamental inequality, as can immediately be seen by adding SA and AL.

The quantum entropy cone is clearly symmetric under all permutations of the
{\sf N}
parties, but its symmetry group is actually Sym
N+1
rather than just Sym
N
. The reason for this enhanced symmetry is the existence of a
purification
for any density matrix, and the fact that for pure states the entropy of a subsystem and that of its complement are equal. Explicitly, given an
{\sf N}
-party density matrix
\rho_{{\sf N}}
, we can construct an auxiliary Hilbert space corresponding to an additional party, for convenience denoted by the number
0
(or by the letter
O
), which we dub the
purifier
, and a pure state
\ket{\psi}
in the enlarged Hilbert space such that

\rho_{{\sf N}}=\text{Tr}_{0}\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}\,.

(5)

Introducing a new index

\underline{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq
[​[
N
]​]
\coloneqq\{0,1,\ldots,{\sf N}\}
,
7
7

7

We use
\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J},\mathscr{K}
to denote subsets of the parties that
do not
include the purifier, and
\underline{\mathscr{I}},\underline{\mathscr{J}},\underline{\mathscr{K}}
to denote subsets of the parties that
can
include the purifier.
we then have for all
\underline{\mathscr{I}}

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}={\sf S}_{{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}}\,.

(6)

where the superscript
c
on
\underline{\mathscr{I}}
indicates complement, or
{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}\coloneqq[\![{\sf N}]\!]\setminus\underline{\mathscr{I}}
.

Given an inequality involving any of the
{\sf N}+1
parties, we can then use this symmetry to obtain a new inequality by permuting the
{\sf N}+1
parties and using (
6
) to remove the purifier when it appears. As an example, for
{\sf N}=2
with parties
A,B
and purifier
O
, we can transform an instance of the Araki-Lieb inequality into an instance of subadditivity as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\sf S}_{A}+{\sf S}_{AB}\geq{\sf S}_{B}\\
\implies\quad&{\sf S}_{A}+{\sf S}_{AO}\geq{\sf S}_{O}\\
\implies\quad&{\sf S}_{A}+{\sf S}_{B}\geq{\sf S}_{AB}\,,\end{split}

(7)

where we obtained the second line by exchanging
B
and
O
, and in the last line we applied the purification symmetry. We leave it as an exercise for the reader to verify that one can similarly map an instance of weak monotonicity to an instance of strong subadditivity and vice-versa.

In the rest of the paper we will find it useful to always exploit this symmetry and write the inequalities of (
1
) as

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\geq{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\qquad

subadditivity (SA)

(8)

\displaystyle{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}+{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\geq{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}+{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}

\displaystyle\text{strong subadditivity (SSA)}\,,

(9)

therefore including all instances of the Araki-Lieb inequality in the set of instances of subadditivity, and all instances of weak monotonicity in the set of instances of strong subadditivity. Throughout this work the purifier will always be considered an ancillary party that
could
be added to the
{\sf N}
-party system to purify a state, but is never really part of an
{\sf N}
-party system. It will merely be viewed as a tool to write instances of AL as instances of SA.

2.2
Patterns of marginal independence and the subadditivity cone

We now recall the notion of a
pattern of marginal independence
(PMI) given in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
. Before giving the formal definition, let us briefly comment on the intuition behind it and introduce some of the geometric constructs that will be used for the definition. Given an
{\sf N}
-party density matrix
\rho_{{\sf N}}
, the
mutual information
(MI) between any non-intersecting pair of subsystems
\underline{\mathscr{I}}
and
\underline{\mathscr{K}}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\coloneqq{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\,.

(10)

The MI is a measure of the total amount of correlation between two subsystems, and it vanishes if and only if the two subsystems are
independent
, i.e., if and only if the
marginal

\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}
of
\rho_{{\sf N}}
factorizes:
8
8

8

More precisely, if
\underline{\mathscr{I}}
or
\underline{\mathscr{K}}
includes the purifier,
\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}
is a marginal of the density matrix
\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}
, where
\ket{\psi}
is the purification defined in (
5
).

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}=\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\otimes\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\,.

(11)

Given an
{\sf N}
-party quantum system, we denote by
\mathcal{E}
the collection of all instances of MI (including the purifier).
9
9

9

It is clear from (
10
) that MI is manifestly symmetric in its arguments, i.e.,
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})={\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{K}}:\underline{\mathscr{I}})
. The collection of MI instances in
\mathcal{E}
takes this into account, so that to avoid over-counting, for every non-intersecting pair
\underline{\mathscr{I}}
and
\underline{\mathscr{K}}
,
\mathcal{E}
will include only
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
or
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{K}}:\underline{\mathscr{I}})
, but not both.
Consider now a bipartition
(\mathcal{E}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{*})
of
\mathcal{E}
, and suppose that we want to construct a state for the system such that all instances of MI in
\mathcal{E}^{0}
vanish, while all the instances in
\mathcal{E}^{*}
do not. One may wonder whether it is useful to define a PMI to be any such bipartition. However, since we are interested in the realizability of a given pattern, defining the complete set of patterns such that most of them are manifestly unrealizable is not the most convenient starting point. Instead, we will make an initial reduction of the full set to a more manageable subset by eliminating patterns that are trivially unrealizable.

One immediate class of such unrealizable patterns stems from the fact that the instances of MI are not all linearly independent. As a simple example, consider the case of
{\sf N}=3
, and suppose that we specify a bipartition of
\mathcal{E}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),\,{\sf I}(B\!:\!C),\,{\sf I}(AB\!:\!C)\}\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{0}\\
&\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)\}\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{*}\,.\end{split}

(12)

Then a density matrix which satisfies these requirements cannot exist simply because of the following identity:

\displaystyle{\sf I}(A\!:\!B)+{\sf I}(AB\!:\!C)-{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)-{\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)=0\,.

(13)

To take such trivial constraints into account, so that we can ignore all bipartitions of
\mathcal{E}
violating linear dependence, it is convenient to use a geometric description. For any MI instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
, the equation

\mathbb{H}:\qquad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0

(14)

specifies a
hyperplane

\mathbb{H}
in the entropy space. The collection of all hyperplanes of this form, for any fixed
{\sf N}
, is a hyperplane arrangement in entropy space, which we now define.

Definition 1

(MI hyperplane arrangement (MIA
N
))
.

For a given number of parties
{\sf N}
, the mutual information hyperplane arrangement is the collection of all hyperplanes

\mathbb{H}
specified by (
14
).

Using this definition,
10
10

10

The reader familiar with the definition given in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
will notice that here we additionally include the hyperplanes where
\underline{\mathscr{I}}\cup\underline{\mathscr{K}}=[\![{\sf N}]\!]
. As we will discuss (cf.
Lemma

1
), these hyperplanes do not correspond to facets of the subadditivity cone, so this inclusion will have no effect on the set of PMIs defined below.

it should then be clear that the bipartitions of
\mathcal{E}
that are compatible with linear dependence correspond precisely to the linear subspaces of entropy space that are intersections of hyperplanes, which are called the
flats
of the arrangement.
11
11

11

By convention, the set of flats also includes the entire entropy space, which can be thought of as the intersection of the empty collection of hyperplanes.
Indeed, the disallowed bipartition (
12
) would never be considered, since (
13
) implies that the intersection of the hyperplanes
{\sf I}(A\!:\!B)=0
,
{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)=0
and
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!C)=0
must lie on the hyperplane
{\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)=0
.

One may then be tempted to define a PMI to be a flat of the MIA. However, this is likewise inconvenient, as most flats correspond to bipartitions of
\mathcal{E}
that are disallowed by another basic constraint, namely SA. Rephrased in terms of MI, SA is simply the statement that MI is always non-negative, and specifies a
halfspace

\mathbb{H}^{+}
of entropy space:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{+}:\qquad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,.

(15)

Consider now the following bipartition of
\mathcal{E}
(again for
{\sf N}=3
):

\mathcal{E}^{0}=\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),\,{\sf I}(AB\!:\!C)\},\qquad\mathcal{E}^{*}=\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{0}\,.

(16)

We leave it as an exercise to the reader to verify that there is no instance of MI in
\mathcal{E}^{*}
which is a linear combination of the instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, so this bipartition corresponds to a flat of MIA
3
. However, notice that (
13
) then reduces to

-{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)-{\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)=0\,,

(17)

and (
15
) then implies that
{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)={\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)=0
. Therefore, the bipartition in (
16
) is ruled out not because it violates linear dependence, but because any non-vanishing entropy vector that satisfies (
16
) and (
17
) cannot satisfy all instances of SA.

To also take into account these implications of SA, we again use a geometric construction. The collection of all halfspaces of the form (
15
) specifies the following convex polyhedral cone.
12
12

12

Convexity follows from the fact that we are intersecting halfspaces, polyhedrality from the finiteness of the set of SA instances, and the fact that the resulting convex polyhedron is a cone is implied by the fact that SA is a homogeneous inequality.

Definition 2

(Subadditivity cone (SAC
N
))
.

For a given number of parties
{\sf N}
, the
subadditivity cone
is the polyhedral cone in entropy space specified by the intersection of all halfspaces
\mathbb{H}^{+}
given by (
15
).

We define a
face

\mathscr{F}
of the SAC
N
as the intersection of the SAC
N
with an arbitrary linear hyperplane such that the SAC
N
is entirely contained in a closed halfspace. Equivalently, we can obtain lower-dimensional faces from intersections of higher-dimensional ones. Two particularly important classes of faces are the
facets
, which are
(\sf{D}-1)
-dimensional faces (corresponding to a region of a given hyperplane
\mathbb{H}
of (
14
) resulting from its intersection with the SAC
N
),
and the
extreme rays
, which are the
1
-dimensional faces. It is conventional to include in the set of faces also a
\sf{D}
-dimensional face, which is the entire SAC
N
.

Using the above definition of a face, we define a PMI as the following linear subspace of entropy space:
13
13

13

This definition is the same as the one used in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
, which improves the original one given in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
to account for SA.

Definition 3

(Pattern of marginal independence (PMI))
.

For a given number of parties

{\sf N}
, a pattern of marginal independence
\mathbb{P}
is the linear span of a face of the
SAC
N
.

One natural specification of a given PMI is the set
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, since this automatically prescribes the bipartition
(\mathcal{E}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{*}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}^{0})
of
\mathcal{E}
. This definition is motivated by the previous discussion regarding the consistency of a bipartition of
\mathcal{E}
, both with linear dependence among the MI instances and with all instances of SA. Indeed, any PMI as defined above is a flat of the MIA, and therefore respects linear dependence. Furthermore, it is guaranteed by construction to contain at least one entropy vector that satisfies all instances of SA, as it contains the corresponding face of the SAC
N
. In fact, the PMIs as defined are
precisely
the bipartitions of
\mathcal{E}
with these properties (see
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
for more details).
14
14

14

Note however that even though PMIs satisfy both linear dependence and SA, most of them are in fact still unrealizable by any quantum state, because they violate SSA or other inequalities. We will return to SSA in §
2.3
.

For any given
{\sf N}
, we will denote the set of all PMIs by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, and similarly, we will denote the set of all faces of the SAC
N
by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}

(when we wish to specify
{\sf N}
explicitly we will include it as a superscript, but to avoid cluttering the notation, we will often keep the
{\sf N}
dependence implicit). As we will discuss in detail in §
3
, these sets have the structure of a lattice,
15
15

15

A lattice is a particular type of partially ordered set that additional structural properties (see
8
).
hence the choice of notation. We will denote a PMI by
\mathbb{P}
, and with a slight abuse of notation, we will use the same symbol to refer to both its
geometric description
as the set of vectors in entropy space that belong to that subspace, and to the corresponding
combinatorial description
as a bipartition of
\mathcal{E}
. It should always be clear from context which particular representation of
\mathbb{P}
we have in mind. In a similar fashion, we will likewise use the notation
\mathscr{F}
for the dual purpose of referring to a (geometric) face of the SAC
N
as well as a (combinatorial) element of the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
.

On the other hand, we will be careful to distinguish a PMI from the corresponding face of the SAC, since geometrically these are different objects. Nevertheless, for any fixed
{\sf N}
, there is the following bijection between the faces of the SAC and the PMIs:

\displaystyle\mu:\quad\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}\;

\displaystyle\to\;\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\;

\displaystyle\mapsto\;\mathbb{P}=\mu(\mathscr{F})\coloneqq\text{span}(\mathscr{F})\,,

(18)

with its inverse given by

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}:\quad\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}\;

\displaystyle\to\;\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\;

\displaystyle\mapsto\;\mathscr{F}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})=\mathbb{P}\cap\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}\,.

(19)

We will prove in §
3.1
that
\mu
is not only a bijective map between
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, but that for any
{\sf N}
it is in fact a lattice isomorphism.

We conclude this subsection with a mathematical observation about the instances of SA
that correspond (when saturated) to facets of the SAC
N
. We defined the SAC
N
above as the intersection of a collection of halfspaces corresponding to some linear inequalities. In such a construction, an inequality corresponds to a facet of the resulting cone if it is
non-redundant
, i.e., if it is not a positive linear combination of other inequalities in the set. We now prove that for any
{\sf N}
, the redundant instances of SA are precisely those of the form
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c})\geq 0
, which reduce to non-negativity of the entropy:
16
16

16

In deriving the r.h.s. of (
20
), we used the fact that
{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}}=0
, since
\underline{\mathscr{I}}{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}=[\![{\sf N}]\!]
comprises of the full system and the purifier.

0\leq{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c})={\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}}-{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}}=2{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\,.

(20)

Lemma 1
.

For an arbitrary number of parties
{\sf N}
, a halfspace
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0
corresponds
17
17

17

In the sense that the span of the facet is the hyperplane
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0
.
to a facet of the
SAC
N
if and only if
\underline{\mathscr{K}}\neq{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}
.

Proof.

We will prove the lemma in two steps.

i) As shown in (
20
), if
\underline{\mathscr{K}}={\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}
, then SA reduces to the non-negativity of the entropy, which (as we already mentioned in
Footnote

6
) is trivially implied by adding (
1
) and (
2
).

ii) Conversely, to prove that any instance of SA with
\underline{\mathscr{K}}\neq{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c}
is not redundant, we will show that it cannot be obtained as a linear combination of other MI instances. Consider a particular instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}\!:\!\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*})
where
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}\not=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}
, and suppose that it can be written as a positive linear combination of a collection of
n
instances
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}\!:\!\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. By the argument in (i), we may without loss of generality assume that
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i}\not=(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i})^{c}
for all
i
. Denoting by
\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
the vector of coefficients of the entropies in the expression (
10
) of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
, i.e., the normal vector to the hyperplane (
14
) with the appropriate orientation, we can write
18
18

18

For all
i\leq n
, if either
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}
or
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i}
includes the purifier, we use the purification symmetry (
6
) to rewrite
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}\!:\!\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})
in terms of the entropies
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{J}}
, with
\mathscr{J}\subseteq[{\sf N}]
. We similarly do this for
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}
and
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}
.

\displaystyle\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}\!:\!\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\;\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}\!:\!\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i}),\quad\text{where $\alpha_{i}>0$\,.}

(21)

Notice that any vector
\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
has precisely two
+1
components and one
-1
component, so summing all the components of each vector on both sides of (
21
) we get

1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\,.

(22)

Consider now the component of
\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*})
corresponding to the entropy of
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}
(or
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}
if
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}
includes the purifier), which must be
+1
. Due to (
22
), the r.h.s. of (
21
) can have a
+1
in the
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}
(or
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}
) component only if every
\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})
also has
+1
in the
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}
(or
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}
) component. This means

\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}\quad\text{or}\quad\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}\quad\forall\;i\,,

(23)

and we can then rewrite (
21
) as

\displaystyle\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*})=\sum_{i:\,\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}}\alpha_{i}\;\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})+\sum_{i:\,\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}}\alpha_{i}\;\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})\,.

(24)

Following the same reasoning for
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}
, we need to impose on (
24
) the analogue of (
23
) for
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}
. However, notice that the assumption
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}\not=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*c}
(along with
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*}\cap\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}=\varnothing
) means that out of the four possible combinations of
(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i},\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i})
, the only pair with disjoint arguments is the one with
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{*},\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{i}=\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{*}
, which means the sum trivializes to all terms having the form of the l.h.s. of (
24
). This completes the proof.
∎

2.3
Realizability of PMIs, strong subadditivity, and Klein’s condition

Having defined a PMI, we now turn to the notion of its realizability. We begin by reviewing the map that associates a PMI to an entropy vector in the SAC
N
from
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
:

\displaystyle\pi:\quad

\displaystyle\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}\;\to\;\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}

\displaystyle\vec{\sf S}\;\mapsto\;\mathbb{P}=\pi(\vec{\sf S})=\bigcap_{\vec{\sf S}\in\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\,.

(25)

In practice, this map associates an entropy vector
\vec{\sf S}\in\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}
to the PMI
\mathbb{P}
of
lowest dimension
that contains
\vec{\sf S}
. Since the map
\pi
is restricted to the SAC
N
, from now on we will always implicitly restrict to vectors of entropy space that belong to SAC
N
(in particular, they are compatible with all instances of SA). The existence and uniqueness of such a PMI should be intuitively clear, and will be formalized and guaranteed by the aforementioned lattice structure of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
in §
3
(see
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
for more details).

Denoting by
\Omega
an arbitrary class of quantum states, which can either be the set of all possible quantum states, or a more restricted class like the set of stabilizer states, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 4

(Realizable PMI in
\Omega
)
.

For a given class of states
\Omega
and a
PMI

\mathbb{P}
, we say that
\mathbb{P}
is
realizable in
\Omega
if there exists a state
\rho\in\Omega
such that
\pi(\vec{\sf S}(\rho))=\mathbb{P}
.

For any given
{\sf N}
, it easy to see that because of strong subadditivity, many PMIs are actually not realizable by any quantum state. The reason is simply the fact that for a PMI
\mathbb{P}
, it can happen that all entropy vectors in the interior of the corresponding face
\mathscr{F}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})
of the SAC violate at least one instance of SSA.

Since SSA is a universal quantum mechanical inequality, it is satisfied by all classes of states of interest. Therefore, we should only focus on the set of PMIs that are not excluded by SSA, leading us to the following definition.

Definition 5

(SSA-compatible PMI)
.

A
PMI

\mathbb{P}
is said to be
compatible
with
SSA
if there exists an entropy vector
\vec{\sf S}
such that
\pi(\vec{\sf S})=\mathbb{P}
and
\vec{\sf S}
satisfies all instances of

SSA
.

In light of this definition, one would like to further characterize the set of SSA-compatible PMIs, and one way to proceed in this direction would be to construct a new entropy cone, carved by all instances of SA and SSA, and consider its faces. However, this would give rise to a rich, yet irrelevant, structure, since there would be numerous faces which would correspond to the same PMI.
19
19

19

As a simple example, consider the
{\sf N}=3
case, where the cone specified by SA and SSA (which is the full QEC
3
) has the extreme ray
\vec{\sf S}=(1,1,1,1,1,1,1)
, i.e., the entropy vector of a density matrix obtained from the
4
-party GHZ state by tracing out any one of the qubits. As one can immediately verify, no instance of SA is saturated by this entropy vector, and its PMI (given by (
2.3
)) is therefore the full space
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
.
Moreover, a moment’s thought reveals that
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
is also the PMI of any vector in the interior of any face of QEC
3
which includes
\vec{\sf S}
on its boundary, since
\vec{\sf S}
is in the interior of the SAC
3
.
Because of this, we will follow another direction, where we replace SSA with a weaker constraint (which we will call “Klein’s condition”) that can be formulated purely combinatorially via the faces of the SAC, without having to introduce any additional inequality.

As we discussed in the previous subsection, the definition of a PMI takes into account the linear dependence among the instances of MI, as well as all instances of SA. However, we have not fully taken into account the fact that, as indicated in (
11
), the MI between two subsystems vanishes
if and only if
the density matrix
factorizes
. For completeness, let us first briefly review why this is the case. The easiest way to see this is via the application of Klein’s inequality
20
20

20

For all positive-definite Hermitian matrices
\rho
and
\sigma
, and for all differentiable convex functions
f:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, the following inequality holds:

\text{Tr}(f(\rho)-f(\sigma)-(\rho-\sigma)f^{\prime}(\sigma))\geq 0\,.

Furthermore, if
f
is strictly convex, equality holds if and only if
\rho=\sigma
. For the quantum relative entropy, one obtains (
26
) and (
27
) by choosing
f(t)=t\;\text{log}\;t
.
to the quantum relative entropy:

{\sf R}\,(\rho\,||\,\sigma)\;=\;\text{Tr}\,\rho\,\text{log}\,\rho-\text{Tr}\,\rho\,\text{log}\,\sigma\;\geq\;0\qquad\forall\;\rho,\sigma\,,

(26)

and in particular

{\sf R}\,(\rho\,||\,\sigma)\;=\;0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\rho=\sigma\,.

(27)

Setting
\rho\equiv\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}
and
\sigma\equiv\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\otimes\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}
, it is easy to see that this translates to (
11
).

Suppose now that the MI instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})
vanishes. We then have

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0

\displaystyle\implies\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}=\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\otimes\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}

\displaystyle\implies\quad

\displaystyle\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{J}}}=\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\otimes\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{J}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}=\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}\otimes\rho_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}

\displaystyle\implies\quad

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}})=0\quad\text{and}\quad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0\,.

(28)

It is crucial to notice that this implication, which is respected by the instances of MI for any entropy vector of a density matrix, is independent from the constraints that we considered when we defined PMIs. Indeed, as was proven in
Lemma

1
, the hyperplane
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0
(for
\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}\not=[\![{\sf N}]\!]
) supports a facet of the SAC, and is therefore by itself a PMI
\mathbb{P}
(i.e., in the specification of this PMI we are assuming that no other instance of MI vanishes, so that in particular
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}})\not=0
and
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\not=0
). Any vector
\vec{\sf S}
in the
interior
of the face
\mathscr{F}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})
is therefore not compatible with the implications in (
2.3
), and it cannot be the entropy vector of a density matrix. Thus, the implication in (
2.3
) is a necessary condition for the PMI to be realizable by a quantum state. Since this restriction follows immediately from Klein’s inequality, we will call it
Klein’s condition
.

Definition 6

(KC-compatible PMIs)
.

Klein’s condition (KC)
for a
PMI

\mathbb{P}
specified by a set
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is the requirement that for all
\underline{\mathscr{I}},\underline{\mathscr{K}}

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\in\mathcal{E}^{0}\quad\implies\quad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{0}\quad\forall\ \underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{I}}\ ,\ \underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{K}}\,.

(29)

If
\mathbb{P}
satisfies
KC
, then we call
\mathbb{P}
a
KC-compatible PMI
.

It is clear in general KC is weaker than SSA, since SSA can also be rewritten as
monotonicity of mutual information
:

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{J}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})-{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,,

(30)

from which (
29
) straightforwardly follows.
21
21

21

More precisely, KC is implied not by a single instance of SSA, but rather by a collection of SSAs and SAs.
In particular, it is immediately obvious that for entropy vectors KC is
strictly
weaker, since (for example) any entropy vector in the interior of the SAC trivially satisfies (
29
), while not all these vectors satisfy SSA.
For PMIs, this means that KC is a necessary condition for SSA-compatibility. However, it is far from obvious that KC is not effectively equivalent to SSA for PMIs, since in this case SSA-compatibility of a KC-compatible PMI
\mathbb{P}
only requires the existence of a
single
entropy vector which satisfies SSA and whose PMI is
\mathbb{P}
. Indeed, one of our results will be that they are not equivalent even for PMIs (cf.
8
). However, as mentioned above, we still view the construction of the set of KC-compatible PMIs as a useful strategy for the derivation of the SSA-compatible ones, and for this reason from now on we will focus primarily on KC rather than on SSA. To this end, it will be useful to first formalize KC in a slightly different fashion, using the language of partially ordered sets. This will be the goal of the next subsection.

We conclude this subsection by commenting on another condition that needs to be satisfied by a realizable PMI, but, as it turns out, is implied by SA. First recall that in quantum mechanics, the von Neumann entropy of a density matrix
\rho
vanishes if and only if
\rho
has unit rank, in which case all subsystems of
\rho
have the same entropy as their complements (cf. (
6
)). Explicitly,

{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}(\rho)=0\qquad\implies\qquad{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}={\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\setminus\mathscr{K}},\quad\forall\,\mathscr{K}\subset\mathscr{I}\,.

(31)

However, this implication follows already from SA, in fact independently from the realizability of a PMI, since

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\setminus\mathscr{K}}-{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}\geq 0\\
-&{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}\setminus\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{K}}+{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}\geq 0\,\end{split}

(32)

implies (
31
) after setting
{\sf S}_{\mathscr{I}}=0
.

2.4
The mutual information poset

In this subsection, we reformulate KC (introduced in
6
) in the language of order theory, by exploiting a certain partial order on the set of MI instances for any fixed
{\sf N}
. We begin by reviewing the definition of a partially ordered set (for more details we refer the reader to
birkhoff1967lattice
and
davey1990introduction
).

Given a set
\mathcal{P}
, a
partial order
on it is a binary relation
\preceq
which is reflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive, i.e., for any
x,y,z\in\mathcal{P}
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&x\preceq x\\
&x\preceq y\;\;\text{and}\;\;y\preceq x\quad\implies\quad x=y\\
&x\preceq y\;\;\text{and}\;\;y\preceq z\quad\implies\quad x\preceq z\,.\end{split}

(33)

A partially ordered set
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
, or
poset
, is a set
\mathcal{P}
with a partial order
\preceq
. A partial order on
\mathcal{P}
gives rise to the following relation
\prec
of
strict inequality
:

x\prec y\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x\preceq y\;\;\text{and}\;\;x\neq y\,.

(34)

Two elements
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
are said to be
incomparable
if
x\npreceq y
and
y\npreceq x
.

We define the
mutual information poset
as follows:

Definition 7

(Mutual information poset (MI-poset))
.

For a given number of parties
{\sf N}
, the
mutual information poset

(\mathcal{E},\preceq)
is the set
\mathcal{E}
of instances of
MI
with the partial order given by
22
22

22

The two alternatives on the r.h.s. are necessary due to the fact that the MI is symmetric, and the partial order that we want to introduce here is insensitive to the ordering of the argument.

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\preceq{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})\quad\iff\quad\underline{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}\;\text{\emph{and}}\;\underline{\mathscr{K}}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime}\quad\text{\emph{or}}\quad\underline{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime}\;\text{\emph{and}}\;\underline{\mathscr{K}}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}\,.

(35)

For given
{\sf N}
the MI-poset has cardinality

{\sf{E}}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\N+2}{3}\,,

(36)

where we have already modded out by the symmetry between the arguments of MI and
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
is the Stirling number of the second kind.
23
23

23

In
Hubeny:2018ijt
;
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
the number of MI instances was
3\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\N+1}{3}
because the trivial instances
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}^{c})
were not included. Indeed one can immediately check that
3\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\N+1}{3}+2^{{\sf N}}-1=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\N+2}{3}
.
The strict inequality

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\prec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})

(37)

is attained when at least one of the inclusions is strict, i.e, when any of the
\subseteq
in (
35
) is replaced with
\subset
.

A convenient way of representing a poset, at least when the number of elements is not too large, is via a
Hasse diagram
, whose construction we now review. In a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
, an element
x
is said to be
covered by
an element
y
, which we denote as
x\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y
, if

x\prec y\qquad\text{and}\qquad x\preceq z\prec y\;\implies\;z=x\,.

(38)

Equivalently, if this implication holds,
y
is said to
cover

x
, and we write
y\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.98332pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x
. Essentially, this means that there are no elements between
x
and
y
distinct from
x
and
y
themselves. The Hasse diagram of a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is a representation of the cover relation between the elements of the poset. It is obtained by first drawing a vertex for each element in the set
\mathcal{P}
, in such a way that for each pair
x,y
, if
x\prec y
then the vertex for
x
is drawn below the vertex for
y
. One then connects the vertices for
x
and
y
only if
x\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y
.
As an example, we have drawn the Hasse diagram of the MI-poset for
{\sf N}=2
in
Figure

1
.

{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO)
{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO)
{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)
{\sf I}(A\!:\!B)
{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)
{\sf I}(B\!:\!O)

Figure 1
:
The Hasse diagram for the MI-poset for
{\sf N}=2
.

Given a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
, a special class of subsets that will be of interest to us are the
down-sets
(sometimes also called
order ideals
). A subset
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
is a down-set if for all
x\in\mathcal{Q}
and
y\in\mathcal{P}
,

y\preceq x\quad\implies\quad y\in\mathcal{Q}\,.

(39)

Using this definition, we can then rephrase KC for PMIs conveniently as follows.

Lemma 2
.

A
PMI
is
KC
-compatible if and only if its set
\mathcal{E}^{0}
of vanishing
MI
instances is a down-set in the
MI
-poset.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is a down-set in the MI-poset, then (
29
) is automatically satisfied given the partial order relation (
35
) for MIs. Hence,
\mathbb{P}
satisfies KC by
6
. Conversely, if
\mathbb{P}
is KC-compatible, then
(
29
)
is precisely the statement that
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is a down-set with partial order relation (
35
), thus proving our claim.
∎

In what follows, given a KC-compatible PMI, we will often refer to the corresponding down-set of vanishing MI instances as the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
.

We conclude this subsection with a few general comments about the structure of the MI-poset, which necessitates us to introduce a few more definitions from the theory of partially ordered sets. A
chain
in a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is a subset
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
that is totally ordered, i.e., it does not contain any pair of incomparable elements, and takes the form (with
q=|\mathcal{Q}|
)

x_{1}\prec x_{2}\prec\ldots\prec x_{q}\,.

(40)

A chain is
maximal
if it is not contained in any other chain. The
length
of a chain is the number of its elements minus one (the number of “jumps”), and the
height

h(x)
of an element
x
is the maximal length of a chain whose greatest element is
x
. In the case of the MI-poset, one can easily verify that the height function is given by

h({\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}))=|\underline{\mathscr{I}}|+|\underline{\mathscr{K}}|-2\,,

(41)

where
|\underline{\mathscr{I}}|
and
|\underline{\mathscr{K}}|
are the cardinalities of
\underline{\mathscr{I}}
and
\underline{\mathscr{K}}
respectively. It follows that for any given
{\sf N}
, the height function of the MI-poset is bounded by
0\leq h(x)\leq{\sf N}-1
.

A poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is said to satisfy the
Jordan-Dedekind chain condition
(JDCC) if given any two elements
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
, all maximal chains with endpoints
x,y
have the same length. It is easy to see that the MI-poset satisfies the JDCC for any
{\sf N}
, since given any two instances
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\prec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})
, one obtains a maximal chain with these endpoints as a sequence of elements obtained by removing a single party at each step. Removing the parties in different orders will yield different maximal chains, but they all clearly have the same length.

A poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is said to be
graded
if there exists a function
g:(\mathcal{P},\preceq)\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle x\succ y\quad\implies\quad g(x)>g(y)

\displaystyle x\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.98332pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y\quad\implies\quad g(x)=g(y)+1\,.

(42)

Since the MI-poset satisfies the JDCC, it follows that it is graded, and in particular that it is graded by its height function (see
birkhoff1967lattice
).

A particularly useful type of a poset, which will play a crucial role in what follows, is a
lattice
, defined as:

Definition 8

(Lattice)
.

A poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is called a
lattice
if for any two elements
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
, there exist both the least upper bound
x\vee y
(called the
join
) as well as the greatest lower bound
x\wedge y
(called the
meet
).

One may then wonder if the MI-poset itself admits this additional structure. One can see that the MI-poset is
not
a lattice for two different reasons. The simpler reason is that it is a finite poset, but it has neither a
top
(an element which is greater than any other element) nor a
bottom
(an element which is less than any other element). This implies that the maximal elements have no join, and that the minimal elements have no meet. However, this can be trivially fixed by formally adding these missing elements, and in the
{\sf N}=2
case illustrated in
Figure

1
, it is easy to check that by adding a formal top and bottom, one does obtain a lattice. However, for
{\sf N}\geq 3
, adding a top and a bottom is not sufficient to yield a lattice because of a deeper issue.

To see why this is the case, it is sufficient to consider the subset of the
{\sf N}=3
MI-poset shown in
Figure

2
. The elements
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!CO)
and
{\sf I}(AC\!:\!BO)
are common upper bounds to
{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)
and
{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)
, but neither is the least upper bound as they are incomparable. Similarly,
{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)
and
{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)
are common lower bounds to
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!CO)
and
{\sf I}(AC\!:\!BO)
, but neither is the greatest lower bound. This is sufficient to prove that the
{\sf N}=3
MI-poset is not a lattice. Finally, notice that the subset in
Figure

2
also appears as a subdiagram of the Hasse diagram of the MI-poset for any
{\sf N}>3
, and any new element that may appear cannot be less than both
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!CO)
and
{\sf I}(AC\!:\!BO)
, since it involves new parties. This implies that the MI-poset cannot be turned into a lattice by adding top and bottom elements for any
{\sf N}\geq 3
. Nevertheless, while the MI-poset does not have the structure of lattice, in the next section we will see that one can build from it useful constructs which do have such a structure.

{\sf I}(AB\!:\!CO)
{\sf I}(AC\!:\!BO)
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!O)
{\sf I}(AC\!:\!O)
{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO)
{\sf I}(A\!:\!CO)
{\sf I}(AB\!:\!C)
{\sf I}(BO\!:\!C)
{\sf I}(B\!:\!AC)
{\sf I}(B\!:\!CO)
{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)
{\sf I}(B\!:\!C)

Figure 2
:
A subset of the
{\sf N}=3
MI-poset, which shows that it is not a lattice. The elements in red are common upper bounds of the ones in blue, but neither is the least upper bound. Similarly, neither of the blue elements is the greatest lower bound of the red ones.

3
The lattice of PMIs satisfying Klein’s condition

In this section, we prove the main results of this work. We begin in §
3.1
by describing the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
of all PMIs, building up from the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
of all the faces of the SAC
N
. This will allow us to show that the subset of KC-compatible PMIs, which we denote by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
, is also a lattice. We then present
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
for
{\sf N}=3
in §
3.2
, where we also discuss its structural properties in detail, and further comment on some of these properties for
{\sf N}=4
in §
3.3
. Using these observations, combined with a canonical construction of
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party PMIs from
{\sf N}
-party PMIs (with
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
), we then prove in §
3.4
some general results about the structure of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
for an arbitrary number of parties. In particular, we prove a theorem about the structure of the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of the KC-compatible extreme rays of the SAC
N
, which largely simplifies their explicit computation. Finally, in §
3.5
, we discuss generalizations of the canonical construction introduced in §
3.4
.

3.1
The set of KC-compatible PMIs is a lattice

3.1.1
SAC lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}

We begin by explaining that the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}^{\sf N}
of all the faces of the SAC
N
for any fixed
{\sf N}
is a lattice, and in particular we will describe the join and meet operations. While we will focus on the SAC, it is true that the set of faces of any polyhedral cone forms a lattice, and we will mainly follow
MR1226888
;
z-lop-95
.

Given
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}^{\sf N}
, we introduce the following partial order on this set:

\mathscr{F}\preceq\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathscr{F}\supseteq\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\,.

(43)

Notice that this is reverse inclusion, rather than inclusion.
24
24

24

Both inclusion and reverse inclusion are common in the literature. We prefer reverse inclusion for consistency with
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
and because it allows for some of the results in later sections to take a more natural form.
To prove that this poset is a lattice, it will be convenient to use the following result from lattice theory.
25
25

25

Of course, the
dual
of
1
, where “bottom” is replaced by “top” and “join” is replaced by “meet” also holds. We will use this dual version later in this subsection.

Theorem 1
.

A finite poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is a lattice if and only if it has a bottom, and the join
x\vee y
exists for every pair of elements
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

See for example Theorem 2.31 of
davey1990introduction
.
∎

This theorem immediately implies that the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
, with partial order (
43
), is a lattice. The bottom is the codimension-
0
face that corresponds to the full cone, and the join is given by

\mathscr{F}\vee\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}=\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\,.

(44)

To see that this is indeed a join, first notice that the face
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}=\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is a common upper bound of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, since
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, and so
\mathscr{F}\preceq\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\preceq\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
. Furthermore, it is the least upper bound, since
\mathscr{F}\cap\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is the largest dimensional face contained in both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, so any other face
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}
contained in both necessarily obeys
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
, or equivalently
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}\succeq\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
.

In what follows, it will be also useful to consider a representation of the faces, which is particularly convenient to describe the join. Since we are dealing with polyhedral cones, each face
\mathscr{F}
can be represented by the set
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}
of extreme rays that generate it,
26
26

26

Any face is the conical hull, i.e., a linear combination with non-negative coefficients, of the extreme rays on its boundary.
and in terms of these sets, (
44
) can then be rewritten as

\mathscr{F}\vee\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\cap\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\prime}\,.

(45)

From now on, by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}^{\sf N}
we mean not just the
set
of all faces of the SAC
N
, but the
lattice
given by the partial order (
43
) on such a set.

While the existence of the join suffices to prove that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
is a lattice by
1
, for our purposes it will be useful to also determine the meet operation explicitly. This is most easily done if we introduce the following notation (for more details see §4.1 of
MR1226888
). To every vector
\vec{\sf S}
in the SAC
N
we can associate an element of
\{0,+\}^{{\sf{E}}}
(with
{\sf{E}}
the total number of MI instances given by (
36
)), called a
sign vector
, such that

\displaystyle\sigma:\quad

\displaystyle\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}\;\to\;\{0,+\}^{{\sf{E}}}\quad

\displaystyle\vec{\sf S}\;\mapsto\;\sigma(\vec{\sf S})=\{\,\text{sign}\,(\,\vec{\sf S}\cdot\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{e}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{e})\,),\,\forall e\in[{\sf{E}}]\,\}\,,

(46)

where
[{\sf{E}}]={1,\ldots,{\sf{E}}}
, and
\vec{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{e}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{e})
is the vector of coefficients of the MI instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{e}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{e})
introduced in the proof of
Lemma

1
, or equivalently the vector normal to the hyperplane (
14
) and directed towards the positive halfspace (
15
).
27
27

27

Obviously we do not obtain negative signs because we are restricting the map
\sigma
to the set of vectors that belong to the SAC
N
.
Also note that even though not all instances of MI in
\mathcal{E}
correspond to facets of the SAC
N
, cf.
Lemma

1
, to avoid introducing additional notation, we do not remove the redundant ones from the MI-poset. It should be clear that this choice has no effect on the following discussion and only affects the explicit form of the sign vectors.

Two vectors in the SAC
N
correspond to the same sign vector if and only if they belong to the
interior
of the same face. We can therefore represent each face
\mathscr{F}
of the SAC
N
by the sign vector
\sigma(\vec{\sf S})
of any vector
\vec{\sf S}
in the interior of
\mathscr{F}
, motivating us to define the sign vector
of a face
as

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (47)

Given the sign vectors

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\prime}\,.

(49)

The fact that this operation is indeed the meet follows from the observation that in the entropy space, the face
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
obtained via this construction is the face of lowest dimension that contains both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
on its boundary.

Whereas the representation in terms of the conical hull of extreme rays was useful in describing the join (
45
), it is no longer convenient for describing the meet. In particular, the meet of two faces is not in general the union of the sets of extreme rays that generate them, since the resulting face may include additional extreme rays.
Conversely, the description of a face by its sign vector is not convenient to express the join. In particular, the sign vector of the join of two faces cannot in general be obtained by
only
assigning a zero component to
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern whenever

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil

or
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern have a zero component, since the face resulting from the join can belong to additional hyperplanes (
14
), and therefore have additional zero components. A simple example of these operations and the role of different representations for the faces is shown in

for a non-simplicial cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(we warn the reader that

3
is included only for the purpose of illustrating the join and meet operations in the lattice of faces of a generic polyhedral cone, and that the figure has no relation to the SAC
N
for any
{\sf N}
).
