Figure 3
:
An example of a
3
-dimensional non-simplicial polyhedral cone and its lattice of faces, ordered by reverse inclusion. We give two equivalent descriptions for each face: the sign vector from (
47
), and the set of extreme rays that generate it. Since the join of two faces is their intersection (cf. (
44
)), it is simply given by the intersection of sets of extreme rays that generate them (cf. (
45
)).
However, in terms of sign vectors, the join is not straightforward to compute, as it is
not
the “union” of the sets of zero components (a component of the sign vector of the join of two faces can be zero even if the corresponding components for both faces are not). An example demonstrating this is
\mathscr{F}_{{}_{\!1}}\vee\mathscr{F}_{{}_{\!3}}
.
On the other hand, the representation in terms of sign vectors is convenient for computing the meet of two faces (cf. (
49
)), which is given by the “intersection” of the sets of zero components (a component is zero if and only if the corresponding components for both faces are zero). However, the meet is not given by the union of the sets of extreme rays that generate the faces, since the resulting face may contain additional extreme rays. An example demonstrating this is
\mathscr{F}_{{}_{\!1}}\wedge\mathscr{F}_{{}_{\!2}}
.

3.1.2
PMI lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}

Having described the join and meet operations in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
, we can now discuss the analogous structure in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, the set of all PMIs. As we explained in
Section

2.2
, for any given
{\sf N}
there is a bijection between the
set
of faces of the SAC
N
and the
set
of PMIs that is given by the map
\mu
defined in (
2.2
) and (
2.2
). Starting from the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, we obtain a
poset
by introducing the following partial order (again we use reverse inclusion, cf. (
43
))

\mathbb{P}\preceq\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathbb{P}\supseteq\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\,.

(50)

Since we have endowed both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
with a partial order, we can then ask how the two posets are related, and intuitively it should already be clear that structurally they are identical. This intuition is confirmed by
Lemma

3
, where we show that the map
\mu
is not just a bijective map between the two sets, but an
order isomorphism
. Given two posets
(\mathcal{P},\preceq_{{}_{\mathcal{P}}})
and
(\mathcal{Q},\preceq_{{}_{\mathcal{Q}}})
, an
order isomorphism
is defined as a bijective map
\phi
from
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{Q}
such that for every
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
,

x\preceq_{{}_{\mathcal{P}}}y\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\phi(x)\preceq_{{}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\phi(y)\,.

(51)

In other words, order isomorphisms preserve partial order, and this is the reason why we can use the same symbol
\preceq
for the partial order in both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
. It should be clear from context which one we mean.

Lemma 3
.

The bijective map
\mu
given in (
2.2
) and (
2.2
) is an order isomorphism between the posets
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{SAC}}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{PMI}}}
, with partial order given respectively in (
43
) and (
50
).

Proof.

For the purpose of this proof, we will need to distinguish the partial orders in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, and we respectively denote them as
\preceq_{{}_{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\preceq_{{}_{\mathbb{P}}}
. For any two faces
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, we have

\mathscr{F}\preceq_{{}_{\mathscr{F}}}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\text{span}(\mathscr{F})\supseteq\text{span}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\quad\implies\quad\mu(\mathscr{F})\preceq_{{}_{\mathbb{P}}}\mu(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\,.

(52)

Vice versa, for any two PMIs
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
we have

\mathbb{P}\preceq_{{}_{\mathbb{P}}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\mathbb{P}\cap\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}\supseteq\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\cap\text{SAC}_{{\sf N}}\quad\implies\quad\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})\preceq_{{}_{\mathscr{F}}}\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\,.

(53)

This completes the proof that
\mu
is an order isomorphism.
∎

The fact that there exists an order isomorphism between the two posets
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
implies that they have the same order structure. Thus, since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
is a lattice,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
is also a lattice, and moreover the join and meet of any two elements in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
correspond to those in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
.

From an algebraic point of view, this means that the map
\mu
respects meet and join, i.e., it is a lattice homomorphism between
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, and in fact is a lattice isomorphism. Explicitly, we have for all
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu(\mathscr{F}\wedge\mathscr{F}^{\prime})=\mu(\mathscr{F})\wedge\mu(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\\
&\mu(\mathscr{F}\vee\mathscr{F}^{\prime})=\mu(\mathscr{F})\vee\mu(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\,,\end{split}

(54)

and since
\mu
is also an isomorphism, we have for all
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{\prime})=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})\wedge\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\\
&\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}\vee\mathbb{P}^{\prime})=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})\vee\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\,.\end{split}

(55)

Using these relations, we can then compute the meet and join for PMIs in terms of meet and join for faces of the SAC via

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mu\left[\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})\wedge\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\right]

(56)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\vee\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mu\left[\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})\vee\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})\right]\,.

(57)

Let us briefly comment on these expressions, starting with the meet (
56
). Consider two PMIs
\mathbb{P}_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}_{2}
written in terms of their sets of vanishing MI instances
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{2}
.
28
28

28

Observe that when we write the PMIs in terms of their sets of vanishing MI instances, reverse inclusion of PMIs corresponds to inclusion of these sets, i.e., both
\mathbb{P}_{1}\supseteq\mathbb{P}_{2}
and (equivalently)
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{0}_{2}
correspond to
\mathbb{P}_{1}\preceq\mathbb{P}_{2}
.

The sign vectors

of the faces

are then given by

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}_{i})_{e}=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{if}\;\;{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{e}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{e})\in\mathcal{E}^{0}_{i}\\
+\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad e\in\,[{\sf{E}}]\,,\;\;i\in\{1,2\}\,.

(58)

Using (
48
) and (
49
) we can write the sign vector of
\mathscr{F}_{1}\wedge\mathscr{F}_{2}
as

(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{F}\hfil$\crcr}}}_{2})_{e}=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{if}\;\;{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{e}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{e})\in\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}^{0}_{2}\\
+\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad e\in\,[{\sf{E}}]\,.

(59)

From (
2.2
) and (
56
), we have

\mathbb{P}_{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}_{2}=\text{span}\,(\mathscr{F}_{1}\wedge\mathscr{F}_{2})\,,

(60)

and from (
59
) it follows that the span of the face
\mathscr{F}_{1}\wedge\mathscr{F}_{2}
is the subspace resulting from the intersection of the hyperplanes (
14
) associated to the MI instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}^{0}_{2}
. We can then write the meet of two PMIs conveniently as

\mathcal{E}^{0}(\mathbb{P}_{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}_{2})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{0}(\mathbb{P}_{1})\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{0}(\mathbb{P}_{2})\,,

(61)

where by
\mathcal{E}^{0}(\mathbb{P})
we denote the set of vanishing MI instances of
\mathbb{P}
.

On the other hand, to determine the join between two PMIs, it is convenient to express the faces
\mathscr{F}_{1}
and
\mathscr{F}_{2}
in terms of their extreme rays, obtaining from (
45
)

\mathbb{P}_{1}\vee\mathbb{P}_{2}=\text{span}\,(\mathscr{F}_{1}\vee\mathscr{F}_{2})=\text{span}\,(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{1}\cap\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{2})\,.

(62)

Notice in particular that unlike the case of the faces, the join of two PMIs is
not
necessarily equal to their intersection,
29
29

29

For example, the intersection of two PMIs could be a linear subspace that is not the span of a face of the SAC, and therefore not a PMI. We could nevertheless geometrically describe the join of two PMIs as the span of their intersection with the SAC.
and in general we only have

\mathbb{P}_{1}\vee\mathbb{P}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{P}_{2}\,.

(63)

Having described the structure of the lattices
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
and their relationship in detail, we now prove the main result of this subsection.

3.1.3
KC lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}

The subset
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
of PMIs that satisfy KC can naturally be seen as a poset with partial order induced by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
. However, it is not immediately obvious that this set has any additional structure. The reason is that in general a subset of a lattice need not be a lattice, since the meet or the join for some pair of elements may not exist. Furthermore, even when it is a lattice, it is not necessarily a
sublattice
30
30

30

A sublattice is a subset of a lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
which is also a lattice, with the same meet and join as the original ones in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
.
since the meet and join operations may change, and several other structural properties may differ substantially from those of the parent lattice. We begin by proving that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
is in fact a lattice for an arbitrary number of parties, and we will devote most of the rest of this work to analyzing its structure in detail.

Theorem 2
.

For any given number of parties
{\sf N}
, the subset
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{\sf N}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{PMI}}}^{\sf N}
of
KC
-compatible
PMIs
, with partial order induced from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{PMI}}}^{\sf N}
, is a lattice. Furthermore, the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{\sf N}
is the same as in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{PMI}}}^{\sf N}
, and is given by (
56
), or equivalently (
61
).

Proof.

For a given
{\sf N}
, consider the poset
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
of PMIs that are KC-compatible, with partial order induced from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
. Notice that the top element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is the trivial subspace that corresponds to the origin of the SAC
N
, and obviously satisfies KC since its
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
is the entire MI-poset, which is clearly a down-set. Hence, since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is a finite poset, it suffices by (the order dual of)
1
to prove that it has a meet, and that it is the same as the one in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
.
Consider two elements
\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
, with respective
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}

\mathcal{E}_{1}^{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{0}
. If both PMIs are in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
, by
Lemma

2
both
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{0}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{2}
are down-sets in the MI-poset. The meet
\mathbb{P}_{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}_{2}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
is given by (
61
), and since the intersection of two down-sets is a down-set,
\mathbb{P}_{1}\wedge\mathbb{P}_{2}
is also an element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. This proves that the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is precisely the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
restricted to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
, completing the proof.
∎

In light of
2
, we now want to determine if, like the meet, the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
is also the same operation as in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, in which case
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
would be a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
. Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
is finite, the join of two elements
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
can formally be written using the meet as
davey1990introduction

\mathbb{P}\vee_{{}_{{}_{\!\!\text{kc}}}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\in\{\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\}^{u}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\,,

(64)

where

\{\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\}^{u}=\{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}:\;\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\succeq\mathbb{P}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\succeq\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\}

(65)

is the set of common upper bounds to
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. Notice that we used a new symbol (
\vee_{{}_{{}_{\!\!\text{kc}}}}
) for the join, since in principle this can be a different operation from the one in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
. On the other hand, because of
2
, we denote the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
by the same symbol that we used for the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
.

To understand (
64
) geometrically, let us first discuss the case of the analogous formula for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
. In that case, the join (least upper bound) is the largest dimensional face on the boundary of both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
(because of reverse inclusion), which is simply their intersection. Furthermore, the set
\{\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\}^{u}
corresponds to the set of all faces on the boundary of both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, and the meet (greatest lower bound) of all such faces is then the smallest dimensional face containing them (again because of reverse inclusion), which is simply the composition of all the faces in
\{\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\}^{u}
. This shows why the version of (
64
) for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
holds.

With this intuition from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
, we now want to understand what changes in the case of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. By the correspondence established in
Lemma

3
between faces of the SAC and PMIs, rather than working with KC-compatible PMIs, it will be convenient to use the corresponding “KC-compatible faces” in the SAC. We denote the lattice of these faces by
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
(which is obviously isomorphic to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
). The main difference now is that the join of KC-compatible faces is not necessarily their intersection, but might be a face
contained
in such an intersection.

Consider two KC-compatible faces
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
which are incomparable.
31
31

31

If they are comparable, the join is just the greater
of the two faces, and (
64
) trivializes.
Their join
\mathscr{F}^{*}=\mathscr{F}\vee\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
is a face
\mathscr{F}^{*}
that may or may not be KC-compatible. If
\mathscr{F}^{*}
is KC-compatible, then the join of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
coincides with
\mathscr{F}^{*}
, and we just explained why (
64
) holds. Hence, suppose that
\mathscr{F}^{*}
is not KC-compatible. We denote by
d
the dimension of
\mathscr{F}^{*}
, and by
\partial_{(\kappa)}\mathscr{F}^{*}
the set of KC-compatible faces on the boundary of
\mathscr{F}^{*}
with dimension
d-\kappa
. Notice that the union of all such sets for
\kappa=1,\ldots,d
is precisely the set defined in the version of (
65
) for KC-compatible faces. Let
\kappa^{*}
be the smallest value of
\kappa
such that
\partial_{(\kappa)}\mathscr{F}^{*}
is non-empty. Notice that since the top of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
is the origin, which is trivially KC-compatible,
\kappa^{*}
is guaranteed to exist. Suppose now that
\partial_{(\kappa^{*})}\mathscr{F}^{*}
contains more than one element. Since the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
are the same, the meet of these elements in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}
is then a KC-compatible face
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}^{*}}
of greater dimension, which must also be on the boundary of
\mathscr{F}^{*}
.
32
32

32

It cannot be
\mathscr{F}^{*}
since
\mathscr{F}^{*}
is not KC-compatible by assumption, and it cannot be a larger face that contains
\mathscr{F}^{*}
because the meet is the composition of faces (cf. (
49
)).
This contradicts the assumption that
\kappa^{*}
is minimal. It follows that
\partial_{(\kappa^{*})}\mathscr{F}^{*}
must only contain a single element, and this is precisely the largest dimensional KC-compatible face contained in
\mathscr{F}^{*}
. Hence, because of reverse inclusion, the single element in
\partial_{(\kappa^{*})}\mathscr{F}^{*}
is the join of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
, which is precisely the l.h.s. of (
64
). Notice that
\partial_{(\kappa^{*})}\mathscr{F}^{*}
is also the composition of all the KC-compatible faces on the boundary of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
, which is exactly the r.h.s. of the version of (
64
) for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\mathscr{F}$KC}}
.

In the above argument we considered the possibility that the PMI of the face
\mathscr{F}^{*}
is not in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. If that is the case, and there exist two KC-compatible PMIs
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{P}\vee\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mu(\mathscr{F}^{*})
, then the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
is
not
a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, since
\mathbb{P}\vee_{{}_{{}_{\!\!\text{kc}}}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
would be a different PMI. As we will see in
Section

3.4
, this occurs whenever
{\sf N}\geq 4
. This also implies that if a face corresponds to a PMI in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
, in general it is not the case that all the faces on its boundary necessarily correspond to PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. Similarly, it is clear that if the PMI of a face is not in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
, the PMIs corresponding to some of the faces on its boundary can still be in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
(e.g., the origin is in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
and is on the boundary of all the faces). It is in principle even possible for a face to correspond to a PMI in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
and yet have none of its facets correspond to a PMI in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. As we will see, this indeed happens for
{\sf N}\geq 4
, and it implies that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
does not satisfy the JDCC for any
{\sf N}\geq 4
.

We conclude this subsection with a comment about the relation between
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
and the MI-poset, further motivating the detailed analysis that we will carry out in later subsections. A
distributive lattice
is a lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
with a particularly nice structure, such that for all elements
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, the distributive laws for the meet and join hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}x\wedge(y\vee z)&=(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z)\\
x\vee(y\wedge z)&=(x\vee y)\wedge(x\vee z)\,.\end{split}

(66)

For any poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
, the set of all its down-sets, with partial order given by inclusion, is a distributive lattice, with the meet and join corresponding respectively to the intersection and union. Since KC can be conveniently formulated in terms of down-sets of the MI-poset, we define the following lattice.

Definition 9

(Mutual information down-set (MID) lattice)
.

For a given number of parties
{\sf N}
, the

mutual information down-set lattice

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{MID}}}^{{\sf N}}
is the of lattice of down-sets of the
{\sf N}
-party
MI
-poset.

\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO),{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO),{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO),{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO),{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO),{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO),{\sf I}(B\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO),{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB),{\sf I}(A\!:\!B)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),{\sf I}(A\!:\!O),{\sf I}(B\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO)\,\}
\{{\sf I}(B\!:\!AO)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),{\sf I}(B\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!O),(B\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!B)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(A\!:\!O)\,\}
\{\,{\sf I}(B\!:\!O)\,\}
\varnothing

Figure 4
:

The Hasse diagram of the down-set lattice

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
of the
{\sf N}=2
MI-poset. Each vertex represents a down-set, labeled by the antichain generating it. The elements in blue are the elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, which for
{\sf N}=2
is a subset of the down-set lattice.
The remaining (white) elements are not in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, because there are additional MI instances which necessarily vanish as a consequence of linear dependence. For example,
\{{\sf I}(A\!:\!B),(A\!:\!O)\}
both vanishing implies
{\sf I}(A\!:\!BO)=0
as well.

For any set
\mathcal{X}
, the lattice obtained by endowing the power set with a partial order given by inclusion is known as the
power set lattice
of
\mathcal{X}
, and we denote it by
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
. As for down-set lattices, the meet and join correspond to the intersection and union respectively, so it follows that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
is a sublattice of
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{E}}
, the power set lattice of the set of MI instances
\mathcal{E}
. Furthermore, since any PMI is specified by its
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}

\mathcal{E}^{0}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{E}}
,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
can be viewed as a subset of
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{E}}
. It is however not a sublattice, since the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
is not (in general) the union of the sets of vanishing MI instances. Nevertheless, since both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
are subsets of
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{E}}
, we have (as sets)

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}\cap\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}\,.

(67)

Furthermore, the order relation in all these lattices is the same as the one inherited from
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{E}}
(cf.
Footnote

28
).

We show in
Figure

4
the simple example of these lattices for
{\sf N}=2
, where we label each element by the
antichain
that generates it.
33
33

33

An
antichain

\mathcal{A}
is a subset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
that is totally unordered, i.e., all elements in
\mathcal{A}
are incomparable. Any antichain
\mathcal{A}
naturally generates the down-set
\mathcal{D}_{\!\mathcal{A}}
via the relation

\mathcal{D}_{\!\mathcal{A}}=\{x\in\mathcal{P},\;x\preceq\mathcal{A}\}\,.

Since the MI-poset is finite, any down-set is generated by an antichain in this fashion.
Finally, we stress that when we represent a PMI by its set of vanishing MI instances
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, all these lattices have the same meet operation, which is simply given by intersection.

Since both the lattices
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
have several nice structural properties, in light of (
67
) it is natural to ask which of these properties, if any, are preserved in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
for an arbitrary number of parties, and if
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
has additional properties which are not inherited from its parent lattices. The main motivation for these investigations is that there exist classes of lattices whose theory is well developed, and for which there are powerful tools to characterize their structure in more detail.

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
of KC-compatible PMIs for
{\sf N}=3

for each dimension

.

This subsection is devoted to a detailed description of the lattice of KC-compatible PMIs for
{\sf N}=3
, which we will denote as
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
. We will look at several structural properties, which we will then analyze for larger
{\sf N}
in the following subsections.
Throughout this subsection we will briefly review
relevant definitions and results from lattice theory; for more details the reader is referred to the standard books
birkhoff1967lattice
;
gratzerbook
;
davey1990introduction
.

d=0
:
\varnothing
d=1

Description of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
:

The lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
has a total of 118 elements. In
Table

1
we report the number of elements for each dimension
0\leq d\leq 7
, and compare it to the number of elements of the same dimension in the full lattice of PMIs
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
. Given the fairly large number of elements, drawing the full Hasse diagram of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is impractical, and we draw instead a more schematic representation of its structure in
Figure

5
. In this diagram, each block corresponds to a collection of PMIs with the indicated dimension. The dashed lines describe the cover relations schematically. When two blocks are connected by a line, we mean that every element in one block covers (or is covered by) at least one element in the other block, and at the same time that every element does not cover (and is not covered by) any element in any unconnected block. The PMIs in a block are described slightly differently from the representations that we have seen earlier. Instead of writing a PMI as the set of all vanishing MI instances
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, or representing it by all the extreme rays generating the corresponding face of the SAC
3
, we describe each PMI
\mathbb{P}
by a collection of elements of a fixed basis of entropy space consisting of 1-dimensional PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
; the said collection comprises of all such 1-dimensional PMIs contained in
\mathbb{P}
.
The fact that this is possible is not obvious, and is in fact a special property of the
{\sf N}=3
case, as we will discuss later.

Each 1-dimensional PMI in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
corresponds to an extreme ray of SAC
3
, and there are two types of such rays in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
. One is the entropy vector of a Bell pair state (denoted by
\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-1.49165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 M -2.98 0 C -2.98 0.99 -3.78 1.79 -4.77 1.79 C -5.75 1.79 -6.55 0.99 -6.55 0 C -6.55 -0.99 -5.75 -1.79 -4.77 -1.79 C -3.78 -1.79 -2.98 -0.99 -2.98 0 Z M -4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 L 4.77 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
), and there are six possibilities, depending on which pair of parties we choose among
\{A,B,C,O\}
. The second is the entropy vector associated to the 4-party perfect state (denoted by
\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to9.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.93608pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 M -2.98 0 C -2.98 0.99 -3.78 1.79 -4.77 1.79 C -5.75 1.79 -6.55 0.99 -6.55 0 C -6.55 -0.99 -5.75 -1.79 -4.77 -1.79 C -3.78 -1.79 -2.98 -0.99 -2.98 0 Z M -4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.77 M 1.79 4.77 C 1.79 5.75 0.99 6.55 0 6.55 C -0.99 6.55 -1.79 5.75 -1.79 4.77 C -1.79 3.78 -0.99 2.98 0 2.98 C 0.99 2.98 1.79 3.78 1.79 4.77 Z M 0 4.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

), which is unique since this state is invariant under any permutation of the parties.

34

This is the entropy vector of the density matrix obtained by tracing out any of the parties from a 4-party state which is maximally entangled for all bipartitions.

. Any vector in the span of these Bell pairs has

, while all instances involving at least three parties are strictly positive. This means that

lies within this subspace (i.e., its addition does not alter the PMI). Therefore since

was not included in our collection, such a collection is not in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
and hence cannot lie in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
. It is clear that adding any more Bell pairs to our collection still retains the redundancy of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(and hence the requirement of its presence in order for the collection to be in

); conversely, we leave it as an exercise for the reader to verify that any fewer Bell pairs would always maintain some vanishing mutual information involving 3 parties (so that

does not lie within this subspace).

Armed with this understanding, we can additionally derive the numbers in the last row of
Table

1
. For example, as we already mentioned above, there are seven 1-dimensional PMIs (six
\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-1.49165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 L 4.77 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and one

from this collection giving the only pair of single parties that have vanishing mutual information.

Finally, notice that since in
Figure

5
we have represented each PMI by a basis, the full collection of extreme rays that generate the face corresponding to a PMI is not always visible. We stress that in general, a PMI in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
can correspond to a face which includes on its boundary extreme rays that generate PMIs which are not in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. This in fact already happens for
{\sf N}=3
, for any face of dimension
d\geq 5
.

The join operation:

Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is a subset of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
(cf. (
67
)), with the same meet operation, it is natural to ask if the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
has any relation to the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
or
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
, and in particular if
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is a sublattice of either
of the other two.

To this end, the representation of the elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
that we have used in
Figure

5
is particularly convenient. Given a PMI
\mathbb{P}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, we will denote its representation using the basis shown in
Figure

5
by
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
. Then one can immediately see from
Figure

5
that given two PMIs
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, their join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
corresponds to their intersection

\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbb{P}\vee_{{}_{{}_{\!\!\text{kc}}}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\widehat{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\cap\widehat{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\,.

(68)

This means that the face corresponding to
\mathbb{P}\vee_{{}_{{}_{\!\!\text{kc}}}}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
must be the intersection of the faces corresponding to
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, implying by (
62
) that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is in fact a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
.

On the other hand,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
. To see why, consider the three join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
with
\mathcal{E}^{0}
corresponding to the principal down-sets of the following three instances in the MI-poset:

{\sf I}(A\!:\!BC)\,,\quad{\sf I}(O\!:\!AB)\,,\quad{\sf I}(C\!:\!BO)\,.

(69)

The join (in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
) of these principal down-sets is just their union. On the other hand, the linear subspace corresponding to the vanishing of all such MI instances is the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
, so the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
of the principal down-sets of the instances in (
69
) is the entire MI-poset. Since the join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not the same as that in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
cannot be a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
.

Atomistic and coatomistic lattices:

In a poset with a bottom element, the elements that cover the bottom are called
atoms
, and a lattice is said to be
atomistic
if every element (other than the bottom) is the join of a collection of atoms. In a finite lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, an element
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
is said to be
join-irreducible
if it is not the bottom element, and the following implication holds

x=y\vee z\quad\implies\quad x=y\;\;\text{or}\;\;x=z\quad\forall\,y,z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}\,.

(70)

Notice that an element is join-irreducible if and only if it covers precisely one element, so in particular all atoms are join-irreducible.

All these notions have a dual version. If a poset has a top element, the elements covered by the top are called
coatoms
, and a lattice is said to be
coatomistic
if every element (other than the top) is the meet of a collection of coatoms. In a finite lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, an element
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
is said to be
meet-irreducible
if it is not the top element, and the following implication holds

x=y\wedge z\quad\implies\quad x=y\;\;\text{or}\;\;x=z\quad\forall\,y,z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}\,.

(71)

Correspondingly, an element is meet-irreducible if and only if it is covered by precisely one element, so in particular all coatoms are meet-irreducible.

For any
{\sf N}
, the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SAC}}^{{\sf N}}
is both atomistic and coatomistic, with atoms corresponding to facets, and coatoms to extreme rays.
35
35

35

Obviously this is true for any polyhedral cone.
Any face can in fact be obtained from the intersection (join) of a collection of facets, or as the composition (meet) of a collection of extreme rays. On the other hand, the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
is neither atomistic nor coatomistic. The reason is that in any down-set lattice, the elements corresponding to the down-sets generated by single-element antichains (also called
principal
) are join-irreducible, and therefore are not in general joins of atoms.
36
36

36

However, if the poset is totally unordered, the down-set lattice is just a power-set lattice, which is atomistic because all join-irreducible elements are atoms.
Furthermore, it is a general fact that in any down-set lattice,
37
37

37

In fact, this is true for any distributive lattice, since by Birkhoff’s representation theorem any distributive lattice is the lattice of down-sets of the poset of its join-irreducible elements
birkhoff1967lattice
.
the poset of join-irreducible elements (with the induced partial order from the lattice) is isomorphic to the order dual of the poset of meet-irreducible elements (this can be seen explicitly for
{\sf N}=2
in
Figure

4
), making the lattice in general not coatomistic.
38
38

38

Again, this holds except when the poset is totally unordered.

In light of (
67
), since in a finite lattice any element can be obtained as the join of join-irreducible elements, or as the meet of meet-irreducible elements, it is then interesting to investigate the structure of the set of such elements for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
, and in particular whether
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is atomistic or coatomistic.
As one can immediately verify from
Figure

5
, in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
there are six atoms, corresponding to hyperplanes of the form

\mathbb{H}:\qquad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0,\quad\text{with}\quad|\underline{\mathscr{I}}|=|\underline{\mathscr{K}}|=1\,.

(72)

As we mentioned above, these are atoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{3}
. The fact they are also atoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
follows from them being the minimal elements of the MI-poset, and therefore each single MI instance in (
72
) is by itself a down-set.

To check whether
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is atomistic, it suffices to note that the join of all the atoms is the PMI generated by the extreme ray

. This immediately follows from (
68
), since as one can see from

,

is the intersection of the collections of generators of all the atoms. The fact that the join of all the atoms is not the top means that the top cannot be the join of any collection of atoms, and so
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not atomistic.

It is also immediate to see that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}

is
coatomistic, since in the representations of the PMIs given
Figure

5
, every element is explicitly a collection of coatoms. Furthermore, it is clear that in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
all coatoms are 1-dimensional PMIs, and that there are no meet-irreducible elements other than the coatoms (as it must be, given that the lattice is coatomistic).

We conclude the analysis of the meet and join-irreducible elements in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
with an intriguing observation that we leave as an exercise for the reader to verify. The join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
correspond precisely to the principal down-sets of the subposet
39
39

39

A subposet of a poset
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
is a subset of
\mathcal{P}
with the partial order induced by
(\mathcal{P},\preceq)
.

of the MI-poset obtained by simply deleting the trivial MI instances given in (
20
). We will comment again on this property in the next subsection, where we discuss the general case of
{\sf N}\geq 4
.

Modularity and semimodularity:

From our previous observations, it is immediately clear that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not distributive, since the poset of join-irreducible elements is not dually isomorphic to the poset of meet-irreducibles (cf.
Footnote

37
). There are however two important generalizations of distributivity, namely
modularity
and
semi-distributivity
, that are interesting to explore. Let us first consider the former.

Modular lattices are defined as those lattices that obey the
modular law
, which states that for any three elements
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
,

\displaystyle x\preceq y\quad\implies\quad x\vee(z\wedge y)=(x\vee z)\wedge y\,.

(73)

A well known result in lattice theory asserts that any lattice is modular if and only if it does not contain a sublattice isomorphic to the
pentagon
lattice
\mathfrak{N}_{5}
. As shown in
Figure

6
,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
contains an instance of
\mathfrak{N}_{5}
as a sublattice (which we leave as an exercise for the reader to show
40
40

40

Notice that the cover relations in
\mathfrak{N}_{5}
need not coincide with those in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
. For example, unlike in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
,
e
covers
b
in
\mathfrak{N}_{5}
(cf.
Figure

6
).
), and therefore it is not modular. There is however a generalization of modularity which is also interesting to explore.

Figure 6
:
An explicit realization of the pentagon lattice
\mathfrak{N}_{5}
as a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, implying that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not modular. Notice that even if
c\preceq d
, we have
c\vee(e\wedge d)=c\vee b=c
while
(c\vee e)\wedge d=a\wedge d=d
, violating (
73
).

A
semimodular lattice
41
41

41

We follow the common convention of calling a lattice simply
semimodular
when it is actually
upper semimodular
. The dual notion of
lower semimodularlity
is introduced below.
is a lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
such that for any
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
,

\displaystyle x\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,y\vee z\;\;\;\text{or}\;\;\;x\vee z=y\vee z\,.

), i.e., such that for any

(75)

A finite lattice is modular if and only if it is both upper and lower semimodular. We will now verify that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is semimodular, thus also implying that it is not lower semimodular.

To see that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is semimodular, it will be convenient to use a different, but equivalent, characterization of semimodularity. A lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
is semimodular if and only if it is graded by its height function (and therefore satisfies the JDCC) and its height function is
submodular
, that is, for any
x,y\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, we have
42
42

42

Similarly, a lattice is lower-semimodular if it is graded by its height function, and the height function is
supermodular
, i.e.,
h(x)+h(y)\leq h(x\vee y)+h(x\wedge y)
for any
x,y\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
.

\displaystyle h(x)+h(y)\geq h(x\vee y)+h(x\wedge y)\,.

(76)

Thus, we should first check that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
satisfies the JDCC. This is clear from

Figure 7
:
A sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, called
\mathfrak{S}_{7}
, showing that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not join-semidistributive. Notice that
a\vee b=a\vee c=d
, while
a\vee(b\wedge c)=a\vee e=a
, violating (
82
).

Semi-distributivity:

We now turn to the other generalization of distributivity mentioned above. Given arbitrary elements
x,y,z
in a lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, the
join-semidistributive law
(SD
∨
) and the
meet-semidistributive law
(SD
∧
) are respectively defined as

SD
∨
:

\displaystyle\quad u=x\vee y=x\vee z\quad\implies\quad u=x\vee(y\wedge z)

(82)

SD
∧
:

\displaystyle\quad u=x\wedge y=x\wedge z\quad\implies\quad u=x\wedge(y\vee z)\,.

(83)

A lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
is
join-semidistributive
if it satisfies SD
∨
. Dually, a lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
is
meet-semi-distributive
if it satisfies SD
∧
.
43
43

43

A lattice that is both meet-semidistributive and join-semidistributive is said to be
semidistributive
(it is not necessarily distributive).

A sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, showing that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is
not
join-semidistributive, is depicted in
Figure

7
. On the other hand,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is meet-semidistributive. To see this, we will use the following characterization of meet-semidistributivity.
44
44

44

This is the order dual of the characterization given in
gratzer2016
for join-semidistributivity.

Lemma 4
.

A lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
satisfies
SD
∧
if and only if for every join-irreducible element
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
, and
x^{*}
the unique element covered by it, there exists a meet-irreducible element
y(x)
which is the unique maximal element
y
such that
y\succeq x^{*}
but
y\nsucceq x
.

Proof.

See Theorem 3-1.4 of
gratzer2016
.
∎

As shown in
Figure

5
, the join-irreducible elements are the atoms and the allowed collections of four

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 M -2.98 0 C -2.98 0.99 -3.78 1.79 -4.77 1.79 C -5.75 1.79 -6.55 0.99 -6.55 0 C -6.55 -0.99 -5.75 -1.79 -4.77 -1.79 C -3.78 -1.79 -2.98 -0.99 -2.98 0 Z M -4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 L 4.77 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.77 L 0 4.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
. This shows that the conditions of
Lemma

4
are satisfied, and that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is meet-semidistributive.

:

and
