Figure 8
:
A schematic representation of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
where the top (origin) and bottom (
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
) elements have been omitted. The blue (green) elements are join-irreducible (meet-irreducible). The red line highlights a cover relation between elements whose dimensions differ by more than one. The solid dot is the join of all the atoms.

The lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is considerably more complicated than
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
, and a graphic representation similar to the one in
Figure

5
is unfeasible. The number of elements for each dimension is given in
Table

2
, where we also show the number of faces of the SAC
4
. One can immediately appreciate how small
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is compared to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
. Nevertheless, its structure is already sufficiently rich to mark a departure from the simple case of
{\sf N}=3
. Specifically, it turns out that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is
not coatomistic
,
does not satisfy the
JDCC, is
not a sublattice
of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
, and is
not meet-semidistributive
.

A cartoon of the structure of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is shown in
Figure

8
, where the top and bottom elements have been omitted. The boxes represent sets of elements with the indicated dimension, and the dashed lines represent the cover relations. For each dimension we have distinguished the join-irreducible elements (in blue) from the meet-irreducible ones (in green), and the elements which are neither join-irreducible nor meet-irreducible are uncolored. Notice that the presence of meet-irreducible elements that are not coatoms immediately implies that, unlike
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not coatomistic.
On the other hand, one can verify that as in the
{\sf N}=3
case, all join-irreducible elements are principal down-sets in the MI-poset.

The solid dot is the
5
-dimensional PMI obtained by taking the join of all atoms, and notice that it is incomparable with all the meet-irreducible elements that are not coatoms. The dashed line in red highlights that there exist
3
-dimensional PMIs that cover
5
-dimensional ones. This in particular implies that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
does not satisfy the JDCC, and in turn, that it is neither lower nor upper-semimodular. The fact that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
can be verified using the knowledge of the full
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
. It suffices to check that there is at least one pair of PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
whose join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
is not an element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
. Indeed, the fact that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not meet-semidistributive can also be verified explicitly using
Lemma

4
. Since these computations are not particularly illuminating, and the explicit description of the counterexamples is complicated by the large cardinality of the sets involved, we omit the details.

3.4
General properties of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
for arbitrary
{\sf N}

Having analyzed the structure of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
in detail, we now turn to the general case. We start by proving a general result regarding a specific embedding of the
{\sf N}
-party lattice into an
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party lattice for
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
. We then combine this result with the previous observations about
{\sf N}=3
and
{\sf N}=4
into theorems that summarize the main structural properties of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
for an arbitrary number of parties, and list a few technical questions that remain open, which we leave for future work.

As we will further explore in the next subsection, given any
{\sf N}
and
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
, there are several sublattices of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
which are isomorphic to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. Intuitively, this has to do with the fact that when we specify a PMI for
{\sf N}
parties, this does not automatically specify which MI instances involving the additional parties should vanish, at least not completely. Here we focus on one such sublattice that will be particularly useful for the proof of
3
below, and for more general constructions see §
3.5
.

The simple construction that we describe here is akin to the one in quantum mechanics, where given an
{\sf N}
-party state we simply add an ancillary system with
{\sf N}^{\prime}-{\sf N}
parties in a pure state, the new parties being both completely uncorrelated with the original system and among themselves. Notice however that while this quantum mechanical intuition is useful to formulate the construction, we are working with PMIs that a priori might be unrealizable, rather than with genuine quantum states.

As usual we denote by
\underline{\mathscr{I}},\underline{\mathscr{J}},\underline{\mathscr{K}},\ldots
the subsets of
[\![{\sf N}]\!]
, and for the new parties we introduce the notation

\Delta=[{\sf N}^{\prime}]\setminus[{\sf N}]\,,\qquad\check{\mathscr{I}},\check{\mathscr{J}},\check{\mathscr{K}},\dots\subseteq\Delta\,.

(84)

A generic subset of
[\![{\sf N}^{\prime}]\!]
that contains some of the original parties in
[{\sf N}]
(including the purifier), as well as new parties in
\Delta
, will be written as
\underline{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{I}}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}\cup\check{\mathscr{I}}
.
45
45

45

In general, the purifier
0
in the larger
({\sf N}+1)
-party system can be different from that in the original
{\sf N}
-party system. Notice that we do not underline the new indices, labeling the purifier of the entire system only with the indices that pertain to the
{\sf N}
-party system. This choice will be convenient below and intuitively it is justified by the fact that the new parties are all uncorrelated and in a pure state.

Given a PMI
\mathbb{P}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
, we would like to map it to a corresponding PMI
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
by specifying its
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
. First, we demand that all MI instances where one of the arguments only involves new parties must vanish, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\,,\quad{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\,,\quad{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\,,

(85)

where
{\sf I}(\cdot:\cdot)^{\prime}
indicates that the MI instance is in the
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party system (as opposed to the
{\sf N}
-party system). Notice that the above equations do not depend on the choice of the PMI involving
{\sf N}
parties. They implement the independence (and purity) of the new parties that we mentioned above, and their implications can be understood geometrically as follows.

Consider the linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}^{\prime}}
specified by (
85
) and notice in particular that

{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{I}}^{c})^{\prime}=0\quad\implies\quad{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}}=0\qquad\forall\,\check{\mathscr{I}}\,,

(86)

where
\check{\mathscr{I}}^{c}\equiv[\![{\sf N}^{\prime}]\!]\setminus\check{\mathscr{I}}
is the complement of
\check{\mathscr{I}}
in the
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party system,
46
46

46

Although
\check{\mathscr{I}}^{c}
includes the purifier, we do not underline the index for the sake of notational simplicity.
and we used (
20
). From (
86
), it follows that the second condition
{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0
in (
85
) becomes

{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\quad\implies\quad{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}={\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\qquad\forall\,\check{\mathscr{I}},\underline{\mathscr{K}}\,.

(87)

Notice that the first condition, as well as the third condition (for
\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}\subset\check{\mathscr{I}}^{c}
), in (
85
) are automatically implied by (
86
) and (
87
), and that together these relations specify an embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
inside
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}^{\prime}}
.

We can now use these relations to rewrite the instances of SA in the
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party system that are not yet specified in the
{\sf N}
-party system. These are all the SA instances of the form
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}\geq 0
and
{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}\geq 0
. We compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}&={\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,,\end{split}

(88)

where we used (
87
) in the second equality. It is similarly straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}={\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,.

(89)

The meaning of (
88
) and (
89
) is that on the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}^{\prime}}
specified by (
86
) and (
87
) (which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
), the new instances of SA all reduce to the set of instances of SA for the
{\sf N}
-party system. These inequalities therefore specify a cone which is simply an embedding of the SAC
N
in
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}^{\prime}}
as a face of the SAC
{}_{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
.

Given an
{\sf N}
-party PMI
\mathbb{P}
corresponding to the face
\mathscr{F}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})
of the SAC
N
, we can then map it to the
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party PMI
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mu(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})
specified by the analogous face
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
of the SAC
N
embedded in SAC
{}_{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
.
47
47

47

With a slight abuse of notation, we denoted the map between faces of SAC and PMIs by
\mu
in both spaces.
This is achieved by including in the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, besides the vanishing MI instances specified in (
85
), also the instances obtained via the following implication due to (
88
) and (
89
):

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0

\displaystyle\quad\implies\quad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\,,\quad{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}}\underline{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\,.

(90)

Notice that this construction is independent from KC, and we can map any
{\sf N}
-party PMI to a new
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party one in this fashion. However, it is immediately clear from (
85
) and (
90
) that KC-compatible PMIs are mapped to KC-compatible PMIs.

We can view the construction we just described as a map
\phi
that maps PMIs from an
{\sf N}
-party system to an
{\sf N}^{\prime}
-party system with
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi:\;\;\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}\;&\hookrightarrow\;\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}\\
\mathbb{P}\;\;&\mapsto\;\;\phi(\mathbb{P})\,.\end{split}

(91)

This map has particularly nice properties, since it is a “cover-preserving embedding” of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
. We will prove this in
Lemma

5
below, but let us first briefly clarify what we mean by this terminology. Given two lattices
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}^{\prime}
, an
embedding

\phi
of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}^{\prime}
is a one-to-one lattice homomorphism from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}
to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}^{\prime}
, and we will say that
\phi
is
cover-preserving
, if

\phi(x)\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\phi(y)\quad\iff\quad x\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y\qquad\forall\,x,y\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}\,.

(92)

With these definitions, we then have the following result.

Lemma 5
.

The map (
91
) is a cover-preserving embedding.

Proof.

From the geometric description discussed above, it is clear that the image
\phi(\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}})
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, implying that
\phi
is an embedding.

To prove that
\phi
is cover-preserving, we need to prove both implications in (
92
). For the forward implication, suppose there are two PMIs
\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
such that
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\phi(\mathbb{P}_{2})
. As
\phi(\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N})
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
, this immediately also implies
\mathbb{P}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathbb{P}_{2}
.
For the reverse implication, suppose that
\phi
is
not
cover-preserving, and consider two PMIs
\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathbb{P}_{2}
, but there exists
\mathbb{P}_{\!*}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
such that
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})\prec\mathbb{P}_{\!*}\prec\phi(\mathbb{P}_{2})
. Notice that we must have
\mathbb{P}_{\!*}\notin\phi(\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}})
, since otherwise
\mathbb{P}_{1}\not\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.27498pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathbb{P}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1,2}
and
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\!*}
be the respective
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
of
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1,2})
and
\mathbb{P}_{\!*}
. Since
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})\prec\mathbb{P}_{\!*}
, we have
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\!*}
,
48
48

48

By
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
we mean that
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}
is a
strict
subset of
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
, that is,
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1}\subsetneq\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
.

which means
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\!*}
includes all the MI instances in (
85
). However, by (
88
) and (
89
), this in turn implies that
\mathbb{P}_{\!*}
obeys (
90
) and is therefore an element of
\phi(\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}})
, contradicting our above assumption and completing the proof.
∎

Having shown that the map (
91
) is an embedding in the lattice sense, we then introduce the following terminology.

Definition 10

(Canonical embedding of a PMI)
.

For
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
, the canonical embedding of a
PMI

\mathbb{P}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}}
with
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}

\mathcal{E}^{0}
into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
is the
PMI
whose
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
is obtained by appending to
\mathcal{E}^{0}
the vanishing
MI
instances given in (
85
) as well as those given by the implication (
90
).

Notice that in the definition of the canonical embedding, we have chosen
not
to permute the parties (for example,
1\in[\![{\sf N}]\!]
is mapped to
1\in[\![{\sf N}^{\prime}]\!]
, etc.). By including such permutations, we can obtain several new embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
; we will come back to this point in §
3.5
.

We can now use
Lemma

5
, and various observations that we made for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
in the previous subsections, to prove several properties of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
for an arbitrary number of parties. These are summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 3
.

For any number of parties
{\sf N}\geq 4
, the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{\sf N}
of
KC
-compatible
PMIs
has the following properties:

(
i
)

It has the same meet as both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{PMI}}}^{\sf N}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{MID}}}^{\sf N}
, but is not a sublattice of either.

(
ii
)

Each atom is a
PMI
with a single vanishing
MI
instance that is a minimal element of the
MI
-poset (i.e., an instance involving only single parties).

(
iii
)

It is neither atomistic nor coatomistic.

(
iv
)

It does not satisfy the Jordan-Dedekind chain condition.
49
49

49

Therefore it is also not upper or lower semimodular, modular, or distributive.

(
v
)

It is neither join-semidistributive nor meet-semidistributive.

Proof.

For (i), we have already seen that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is a lattice with the same meet as
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
(cf.
2
) and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{\sf N}
(cf. (
67
)). We have also seen in §
3.2
that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{3}
since they have different joins, and likewise in §
3.3
that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
. Now for
{\sf N}\geq 4
, consider the canonical embedding of two PMIs
\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
, whose join is different from their join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
, into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. Letting
\mathbb{P}_{3}=\mathbb{P}_{1}\vee_{\text{kc}}\mathbb{P}_{2}
, it follows
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})\vee_{\text{kc}}\phi(\mathbb{P}_{2})=\phi(\mathbb{P}_{3})
. Furthermore, it is clear that the map
\phi
is also an embedding of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{4}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}
, implying that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1}\vee\mathbb{P}_{2}=\mathbb{P}_{3}^{\prime}\not=\mathbb{P}_{3}\quad\implies\quad\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})\vee\phi(\mathbb{P}_{2})=\phi(\mathbb{P}_{3}^{\prime})\not=\phi(\mathbb{P}_{3})\,,

(93)

so the join of
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{1})
and
\phi(\mathbb{P}_{2})
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{\sf N}
is different from their join in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. By a similar argument,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is not a sublattice of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{\sf N}
either.

For (ii), notice that the down-sets generated by the minimal elements of the MI-poset are precisely the down-sets with cardinality one. Since their corresponding hyperplanes are PMIs, they are the atoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
.

For (iii), consider the pure quantum state
|\!\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to9.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.93608pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 M -2.98 0 C -2.98 0.99 -3.78 1.79 -4.77 1.79 C -5.75 1.79 -6.55 0.99 -6.55 0 C -6.55 -0.99 -5.75 -1.79 -4.77 -1.79 C -3.78 -1.79 -2.98 -0.99 -2.98 0 Z M -4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.77 0 M 6.55 0 C 6.55 0.99 5.75 1.79 4.77 1.79 C 3.78 1.79 2.98 0.99 2.98 0 C 2.98 -0.99 3.78 -1.79 4.77 -1.79 C 5.75 -1.79 6.55 -0.99 6.55 0 Z M 4.77 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.77 M 1.79 4.77 C 1.79 5.75 0.99 6.55 0 6.55 C -0.99 6.55 -1.79 5.75 -1.79 4.77 C -1.79 3.78 -0.99 2.98 0 2.98 C 0.99 2.98 1.79 3.78 1.79 4.77 Z M 0 4.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.77 M 1.79 -4.77 C 1.79 -3.78 0.99 -2.98 0 -2.98 C -0.99 -2.98 -1.79 -3.78 -1.79 -4.77 C -1.79 -5.75 -0.99 -6.55 0 -6.55 C 0.99 -6.55 1.79 -5.75 1.79 -4.77 Z M 0 -4.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.77 0 L 4.77 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.77 L 0 4.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}~\!\!\!\rangle_{1234}\otimes|0\rangle_{5}\otimes\cdots\otimes|0\rangle_{{\sf N}}
. Its PMI
\mathbb{P}
is greater than or equal to the join of all the atoms, since all the MIs between two single parties vanish. As
\mathbb{P}
is clearly not the top element (which is the state with every MI instance vanishing), this means the top element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is not the join of all the atoms, so the lattice is not atomistic.

Next, to prove that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is not coatomistic, recall that we showed in §
3.3
that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not coatomistic, so there exists a PMI
\mathbb{P}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
that is not the meet of a collection of coatoms in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
. Now consider its canonical embedding
\phi(\mathbb{P})\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. Notice that any coatom
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
greater than
\phi(\mathbb{P})
must obey (
85
) and hence is part of the canonical embedding of the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
, and
\phi(\mathbb{P})
by assumption cannot be the meet of such coatoms. This proves
\phi(\mathbb{P})
is not the meet of an arbitrary collection of coatoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, and so
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is not coatomistic.

For (iv), we know from §
3.3
that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
does not satisfy the JDCC, and since the canonical embedding is cover-preserving (cf.
Lemma

5
), it follows that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
does not either.

For (v), we have seen in §
3.2
that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{3}
is not join-semidistributive because it contains the sublattice
\mathfrak{S}_{7}
(cf.
Figure

7
). This automatically implies that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is likewise not join-semidistributive for any
{\sf N}>3
since it also contains the same sublattice. Furthermore, in §
3.3
we used
Lemma

4
to verify that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not meet-semidistributive. As for join-semidistributivity, it is a general result of lattice theory that a lattice is meet-semidistributive if and only if it does not contain a sublattice isomorphic to one of several forbidden lattices (see for example
gratzer2016
). Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
is not meet-semidistributive, it contains such a sublattice, and therefore so does
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
for any
{\sf N}\geq 4
, which means
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is also not meet-semidistributive.
∎

Despite the fact that
3
above proves that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
does not belong to any of the widely studied classes of lattices, we prove below two interesting properties of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
.

Theorem 4
.

The set of join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{\sf N}
includes all those of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{MID}}}^{\sf N}
that are not principal down-sets of maximal elements of the
MI
-poset.

Proof.

Consider a non-maximal element of the MI-poset
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
(so
\underline{\mathscr{K}}\not=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{c}
), and let
\mathcal{E}^{0}
be its principal down-set. Because the join of two elements in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{\sf N}
is given by their union, any principal down-set is a join-irreducible element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
. We now want to show that
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI
\mathbb{P}
, and that
\mathbb{P}
is also join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
.

To show that
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI
\mathbb{P}
, consider the quantum state

\ket{\pmi}=\bigotimes_{\forall\{i,k\}\,\notin\,\Gamma}\ket{\bpg}_{ik}\,,

(94)

where
\ket{\bpg}_{ik}
is a Bell pair between parties
i
and
k
, and
\Gamma
is the set

\Gamma=\{\{i,k\}:i\in\underline{\mathscr{I}},k\in\underline{\mathscr{K}}\}\,.

(95)

The
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of the PMI of
\ket{\pmi}
is precisely
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, and since
\ket{\pmi}
is a quantum state, it automatically follows that
\mathbb{P}
is in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
.

We now want to show that
\mathbb{P}
is join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. Let
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
be the unique element in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{0}\mathrel{\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to5.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.98332pt\lower-2.98332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.36 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -3.57 -3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
, which is obtained from
\mathcal{E}^{0}
by simply removing the generating instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
. Notice that since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is a subset of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
with the induced partial order, to show that
\mathbb{P}
is join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
it suffices to show that
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
is the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI
\mathbb{P}_{*}
that is also in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. If
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
is the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI, this PMI is in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
by construction, so suppose that
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
is not the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI. Then it must be by linear dependence and SA that the MI instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
vanishing implies that additional MI instances must also vanish. However, if any instance other than
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
must be added to
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
to obtain a PMI, it must also be added to
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is already a PMI. The only remaining possibility then is that linear dependence and SA imply that
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
, and nothing else, must be added to
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
. We now proceed to show that this is also not possible.

First notice that every MI instance in
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is of the form
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})
for
\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{I}}
and
\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{K}}
. Thus, if we write each MI instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{E}^{0}
in terms of the entropies, they each contain a term that is the entropy
{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{\prime}\underline{\mathscr{K}}^{\prime}}
, which is not present in any of the other MI instances (notice that this is true only if
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is not a maximal element of the MI-poset, since otherwise
{\sf S}_{\underline{\mathscr{I}}\underline{\mathscr{K}}}=0
). This means there cannot exist a set of MI instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}
that are linearly dependent, and in particular that
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
cannot be required to vanish by the fact that all MI instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
vanish.

The only remaining possibility is that the vanishing of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is required by SA. To clarify what we mean by this statement, denote by
\mathbb{S}_{*}
the linear subspace of entropy space that is the intersection of all the hyperplanes corresponding to the vanishing MI instances in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
. If
\mathbb{S}_{*}
is spanned by a face of the SAC, it is a PMI, so the possibility we are contemplating is that this is not the case, but rather that the largest PMI contained in
\mathbb{S}_{*}
is a proper subspace obtained by imposing the SA instance
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0
. However, imposing
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0
simply partitions
\mathbb{S}_{*}
into two codimension-0 regions in
\mathbb{S}_{*}
. Therefore, to restrict the solution of the system of inequalities corresponding to the instances of SA which are not in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
to a lower-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{S}_{*}
, one needs at least two additional linear inequalities to be saturated. This contradicts our above hypothesis that
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is the only additional SA that must be saturated, and thus completes the proof that
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
is the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI
\mathbb{P}_{*}
, and that
\mathbb{P}
is join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
.

Finally, consider the case where
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is a maximal element of the MI poset (so
\underline{\mathscr{K}}=\underline{\mathscr{I}}^{c}
). Its principal down-set
\mathcal{E}^{0}
still corresponds to a PMI
\mathbb{P}
, since the construction (
94
) gives a quantum state whose PMI has
\mathcal{E}^{0}
as its
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
. However, while
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is still a join-irreducible element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
,
\mathbb{P}
is no longer join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. To prove this, first notice that the down-set
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
obtained from
\mathcal{E}^{0}
by removing the top element
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is not the vanishing down-set of a PMI. This follows from the linear dependence relation

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{1})+{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{2})-\,{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})=0\,,

(96)

where
\{\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{1},\underline{\mathscr{K}}_{2}\}
is an arbitrary bipartition of
\underline{\mathscr{K}}
. Because the first two MI instances in (
96
) are in
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
but not the third, the down-set
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
is not the vanishing down-set of a PMI. In the MI-poset, let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of MI instances covered by
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
, and let
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}^{0}_{|\mathcal{C}|}
be the principal down-sets generated by the elements of
\mathcal{C}
. As we showed above, the principal down-sets
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{i}
all correspond to the vanishing down-sets of PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. The join of all these PMIs is a new PMI whose
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
is the smallest down-set that corresponds to a PMI and also includes the union of all the down-sets
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{i}
. However, notice that the union of these down-sets is precisely
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
, so the smallest down-set that both contains
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{*}
and corresponds to a PMI is the principal down-set
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, since by (
96
) we must at least include
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
in the down-set. This proves that
\mathbb{P}
, whose vanishing down-set is the principal down-set of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
, can be obtained as the join of a collection of PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
and is thus not join-irreducible.
∎

In
4
, we determined that the set of join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
contains a specific subset of those in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{\sf N}
. Naturally, it would be interesting to know if these are all the join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. If this is true, then the set of join-irreducible elements would be isomorphic to the subposet of the MI-poset where the maximal elements have been removed. We checked explicitly that this is true for
{\sf N}=3
and
{\sf N}=4
, and we phrase this formally as a question for arbitrary
{\sf N}
below.

Question 1
.

Is the set of join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}
, with the induced partial order, isomorphic to the subposet of the
MI
-poset obtained by removing the maximal elements?

We conclude this subsection with an additional result about the order relation between atoms and coatoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
and a few comments about its usefulness for explicit computations.

.

Given any coatom
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
that is not a permutation of the canonical embedding of an

PMI

Proof.

Let us denote by
\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{ij}
the PMI of a quantum state which is the tensor product of a Bell pair between parties
i,j\in[\![{\sf N}]\!]
and arbitrary pure, uncorrelated states for the remaining
{\sf N}-1

\mathcal{E}^{0}_{ij}
of vanishing instances of
\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{ij}
is the complement of the
principal up-set
50
50

50

Similarly to principal down-sets, these are up-sets generated by a single MI instance.
of
{\sf I}(i:j)
, which means
\mathcal{E}^{0}_{ij}
contains all the atoms except the one where
{\sf I}(i:j)=0
. This proves the second claim in the theorem. In addition, any PMI
\mathbb{P}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{0}\subset\mathcal{E}^{0}_{ij}
cannot be a coatom, and by permutations, the same implication obviously holds for any
i,j
. Thus, for any coatom
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
which is not of the form
\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{ij}
for some
i,j
, its vanishing set of MI instances
\mathcal{E}^{0}
must include all atoms, and so the first claim is also proven.
∎

Lastly, note that although every 1-dimensional KC-compatible PMI is a coatom of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
with
{\sf N}=3,4
are indeed all 1-dimensional PMIs. It is then intriguing to speculate that this is true for arbitrary
{\sf N}
, and we pose the following question, which we leave for future investigation.

Question 2
.

Are all coatoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{\sf N}

1
-dimensional
PMI
s?

3.5
Constructing
{\sf N}
-party PMIs from fewer parties

In the previous subsection, we have seen how given the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
(for any
{\sf N}
) we can embed it inside the bigger lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
for any
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
. This embedding has particularly nice properties (cf.
Lemma

5
) that will be useful for the proof of
6
, but it is highly non-unique. The goal of this subsection is to discuss the multitude of different possible embeddings, and how they can be “combined” to construct a part of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
. The main motivation for this analysis is that in the construction of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
, we may assume complete knowledge of the lattices involving fewer parties, and we would then like to focus on the “genuinely new” elements.

As we already mentioned, the first obvious reason why the canonical embedding is non-unique has to do with permutations of the parties. In (
85
) and (
90
), each party labeled by an element of
[\![{\sf N}]\!]
was given the same label in
[\![{\sf N}^{\prime}]\!]
, but in general this does not have to be the case. Indeed, given
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
and
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
, one can obtain different embeddings into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
by first constructing the canonical embedding, and then simply permuting the parties.

To describe more general embeddings, it will be convenient to use a standard lattice construction, which we will now briefly review. Given two lattices
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{1}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{2}}
, their
product

\mathfrak{L}_{\text{}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\text{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{2}}
is obtained by first constructing the Cartesian product of the two sets
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{1}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{2}}
, and then specifying the following partial order:
51
51

51

The generalization to an arbitrary number of factors is obvious.

(x_{1},y_{1})\preceq(x_{2},y_{2})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x_{1}\preceq x_{2}\;\;\text{and}\;\;y_{1}\preceq y_{2}\,.

(98)

For PMIs that are realizable in quantum mechanics, we can intuitively think of this construction as follows. Suppose we have two quantum systems with
{\sf N}_{1}
and
{\sf N}_{2}
parties. As long as the two systems are independent, we are free to construct a quantum state for each one, and realize any quantum mechanical PMI in both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}
. The tensor product of two such states will then realize a PMI in the larger lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, with
{\sf N}={\sf N}_{1}+{\sf N}_{2}
. Since the choices for the two systems are independent, we have an embedding of the subset of quantum mechanically realizable PMIs of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}
inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
.

While this construction is clear in quantum mechanics, not all KC-compatible PMIs are a priori realizable, and we should therefore reformulate it purely in the language of PMIs without relying on quantum states. We will soon discuss this reformulation precisely, but first it is instructive to consider the simple example
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{2}
, where all KC-compatible PMIs are realizable by quantum states and we can conveniently use the construction mentioned above.

{\sf I}(A:O)

(a)

\bullet
\mathbb{R}^{1}

(b)

\{{\sf I}(A:O)
}
\varnothing

(c)

Figure 9
:
Various constructs for
{\sf N}=1
: (a) the MI poset, (b) SAC
1
, and (c)
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
.

Let us first clarify how to construct the trivial
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
. For
{\sf N}=1
, the MI-poset contains the single instance
{\sf I}(A:O)
, and SAC
1
, which is a cone in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, reduces to a single ray. There are only two faces in SAC
1
: the ray, and the origin. Clearly the corresponding PMIs are KC-compatible and quantum mechanically realizable, and in fact they simply distinguish mixed from pure states (all the constructs for
{\sf N}=1
are shown in
Figure

9
).

The lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
, is shown in
Figure

10(a)
, where for each element we also show an explicit realization by a quantum state. Notice that these are 2-party states, and that each corresponds to a unique
{\sf N}=2
PMI. In particular, all correlations between the parties
A
and
B
and the purifier
O
are completely fixed by the specification of which subsystems are pure or mixed. The three possible embeddings of this lattice inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{2}
, obtained by permuting the parties, are shown in
Figure

10(b)
,
10(c)
and
10(d)
.
