Figure 10
:
The three possible embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
into
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{2}
. In (a), we give a detailed description of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
(one factor in orange and one in violet), with an explicit realization of the element by quantum states (all density matrices are assumed to be mixed). Notice that the correlation between
A,B
and the purifier
O
(not shown) is completely fixed by the specification of which subsystems are pure and which are mixed. In (b), (c), and (d), we illustrate the three possible embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{2}
, which are described by
6
.

For an arbitrary number of parties
{\sf N}
, the embedding of these product lattices inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
are described by the following result.

Theorem 6
.

For any given
{\sf N}
, and
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1},\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2}\subset[\![{\sf N}]\!]
with
{\sf N}_{1}=|\mathscr{I}_{1}|
and
{\sf N}_{2}=|\mathscr{I}_{2}|
, such that
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1}\cap\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2}=\varnothing
and
{\sf N}_{1}+{\sf N}_{2}={\sf N}
, the subset of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}}
of
PMI
s whose
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
include the principal down-set of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1}:\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2})^{\prime}
, with the induced partial order, is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the theorem in the case of
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1}=[{\sf N}_{1}]
and
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2}=[{\sf N}]\setminus[{\sf N}_{1}]
(where neither
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1}
nor
\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2}
contain the purifier), since any other case can be reduced to it by a permutation of the parties. We will proceed in a similar fashion as in the description of the canonical embedding in §
3.4
. We denote by
\hat{\mathscr{I}},\hat{\mathscr{J}},\hat{\mathscr{K}}
the subsets of
[{\sf N}_{1}]
, and by
\check{\mathscr{I}},\check{\mathscr{J}},\check{\mathscr{K}}
those of
[{\sf N}]\setminus[{\sf N}_{1}]
. As usual, we will continue the convention where the underlined version of these indices can include the purifier 0.

For a PMI such that its
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
includes the principal down-set of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{1}:\underline{\mathscr{I}}_{2})
, we have

{\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}=0\quad\implies\quad{\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}={\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{K}}}\qquad\forall\,\hat{\mathscr{I}},\check{\mathscr{K}}\,.

(99)

We now proceed to show that given (
99
), all the MI instances in a PMI
\mathbb{P}
in the
{\sf N}
-party system reduce to those in the smaller
{\sf N}_{1}
and
{\sf N}_{2}
-party systems. Obviously, we have

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\hat{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}:\hat{\underline{\mathscr{K}}})^{\prime}={\sf I}(\hat{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}:\hat{\underline{\mathscr{K}}})\geq 0\,,\qquad{\sf I}(\check{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}:\check{\underline{\mathscr{K}}})^{\prime}={\sf I}(\check{\underline{\mathscr{I}}}:\check{\underline{\mathscr{K}}})\geq 0\,,

(100)

so it suffices to focus on the MI instances involving parties from both the
{\sf N}_{1}
-party system and
{\sf N}_{2}
-party system. Proceeding as in (
88
), it is straightforward to evaluate using (
99
) the cases not involving the purifier
0
:

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0

(101)

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0

(102)

\displaystyle{\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}})+{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,.

(103)

The cases involving the purifier
0
are a little more tricky. However, we will show that the MI instances in the larger
{\sf N}
-party system reduce in the same manner to MI instances in the smaller systems. It is convenient to use the notation
{\hat{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}\equiv[{\sf N}_{1}]\setminus\hat{\mathscr{I}}
, which is a subset of parties in
[{\sf N}_{1}]
, and similarly
{\check{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}\equiv([{\sf N}]\setminus[{\sf N}_{1}])\setminus\check{\mathscr{I}}
, which is a subset of parties in
[{\sf N}]\setminus[{\sf N}_{1}]
.
52
52

52

Note that
{\hat{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}
(
{\check{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}
) is almost the complement of
\hat{\mathscr{I}}
(
\check{\mathscr{I}}
) in the
{\sf N}_{1}
-party (
{\sf N}_{2}
-party) subsystem, except that we are ignoring the purifier.
It then follows
53
53

53

In the rest of the proof we denote by
0_{1}
and
0_{2}
the purifiers of the smaller subsystems.

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:0\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}&={\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{0\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{0\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{{\hat{\mathscr{K}}^{\dagger}}{\check{\mathscr{K}}^{\dagger}}}-{\sf S}_{(\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}})^{\dagger}{\check{\mathscr{K}}^{\dagger}}}\\
&={\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{{\hat{\mathscr{K}}^{\dagger}}}-{\sf S}_{(\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}})^{\dagger}}\\
&={\sf I}(\hat{\mathscr{I}}:0_{1}\hat{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,,\end{split}

(104)

where we first used purification symmetry in the full
{\sf N}
-party system, then (
99
), and finally applied purification once more but now in the smaller
{\sf N}_{1}
-party system. Applying identical arguments, we also derive

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:0\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}&={\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:0_{2}\check{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,.\end{split}

(105)

The equations (
101
), (
102
), (
104
), and (
105
) (note that (
103
) is redundant) demonstrate how some of the new MI instances of the
{\sf N}
-party system reduces to simply the MI instances of the
{\sf N}_{1}
-party and
{\sf N}_{2}
-party systems. There are still a few MI instances in the
{\sf N}
-party system that we have to check whether they can be written in terms of the
{\sf N}_{1}
and
{\sf N}_{2}
-party MI instances. We demonstrate one such MI instance computation below:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf I}(0\hat{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}})^{\prime}&={\sf S}_{0\hat{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{0\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf S}_{{\hat{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}\check{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{K}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{(\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}})^{\dagger}\check{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf S}_{{\hat{\mathscr{I}}^{\dagger}}}+{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}}+{\sf S}_{\hat{\mathscr{K}}}+{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{K}}}-{\sf S}_{(\hat{\mathscr{I}}\hat{\mathscr{K}})^{\dagger}}-{\sf S}_{\check{\mathscr{I}}\check{\mathscr{K}}}\\
&={\sf I}(0_{1}\hat{\mathscr{I}}:\hat{\mathscr{K}})+{\sf I}(\check{\mathscr{I}}:\check{\mathscr{K}})\geq 0\,,\end{split}

(106)

where we utilized purification symmetry (in both the larger
{\sf N}
-party system and in the smaller
{\sf N}_{1}
-party system) and (
99
). We leave the reader to check that all other MI instances in the
{\sf N}
-party system similarly reduce to a positive sum of two MI instances involving the
{\sf N}_{1}
and
{\sf N}_{2}
-party systems (analogous to (
103
)).

Geometrically, (
99
) means that we have an embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{2}}
inside
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}}
, while the SA instances in (
100
), (
101
), (
102
), (
104
), and (
105
) specify two cones on
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{1}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{2}}
which are isomorphic respectively to SAC
{}_{{\sf N}_{1}}
and SAC
{}_{{\sf N}_{2}}
(the SA instances in (
103
) and (
106
) are redundant). Globally then, we have a cone in
\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\sf{D}_{2}}
which is the direct sum of SAC
{}_{{\sf N}_{1}}
and SAC
{}_{{\sf N}_{2}}
. The set of faces of this cone is the Cartesian product of the sets of faces the two cones, and the partial order of this set is given by (
98
), completing the proof.
∎

The theorem above describes the possible embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}
inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
for
{\sf N}={\sf N}_{1}+{\sf N}_{2}
. An alternative way of understanding it is that it reveals the structure of the set of embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
for all
{\sf N}<{\sf N}^{\prime}
, where each element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}-{\sf N}}
corresponds to one such embedding. From this perspective, the canonical embedding of
Lemma

5
can be seen as corresponding to the embedding of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
specified by the top element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}-{\sf N}}
. A similar reasoning also clarifies what are the embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}
inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
for
{\sf N}>{\sf N}_{1}+{\sf N}_{2}
, since one can simply construct
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{2}}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
, with
{\sf N}^{\prime}={\sf N}-{\sf N}_{1}-{\sf N}_{2}
. Indeed, starting from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, one can apply
6
repeatedly to obtain the embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}_{n}}
inside
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\sf N}_{i}={\sf N}
.

We conclude this section with a comment about how
6
can be utilized to make the derivation of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
more efficient. Suppose that we know
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}-1}
, and we want to construct
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. Using
6
we can construct the possible embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}-1}\times\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, including all possible permutations of the parties. Notice that this construction automatically includes all the embeddings of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}^{\prime}}
in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, with
{\sf N}^{\prime}<{\sf N}-1
, since these lattices are already embedded in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}-1}
. We can then take the meet for all possible collections of the elements obtained via these embeddings to construct a large portion of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. For example, in the
{\sf N}=2
case, this procedure yields the entire
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{2}
starting from
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{1}
, and in this sense there are no “genuinely
2
-party” PMIs. This can be seen in
Figure

10
.

It would be interesting to investigate possible generalizations of
6
, and to analyze more systematically the necessary conditions that must be imposed on the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
of a PMI such that it cannot be obtained from fewer party PMIs via these type of constructions. We leave this problem for future work.

4
Comments on the realizability of KC-compatible PMIs

In this short section, we make a few comments on the realizability of PMIs that are KC-compatible, as well as on the relation between KC and SSA. In
Section

2.3
, we defined realizability of PMIs for an arbitrary class of quantum states
\Omega
(cf.
4
). Here we make a few more assumptions about
\Omega
. Specifically, we assume that for any
{\sf N}
,
\Omega
includes the completely uncorrelated state

\ket{0}_{1}\otimes\ket{0}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{0}_{{\sf N}}\,,

(107)

and that it is
closed under tensor products
, i.e., the following implication holds:
54
54

54

Notice that we are fixing the number of parties
{\sf N}
even if the tensor product of the density matrices is an operator acting on a larger Hilbert space. This simply amounts to enlarging the dimension of the Hilbert space for each party in
[{\sf N}]
.

\forall\,\rho_{{\sf N}},\rho_{{\sf N}}^{\prime}\in\Omega\quad\implies\quad\rho_{{\sf N}}\otimes\rho_{{\sf N}}^{\prime}\in\Omega\,.

(108)

With these (very mild) assumptions, we then have the following result.

Theorem 7
.

For any given
{\sf N}
, the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\Omega$}}^{{\sf N}}
of
PMI
s which are realizable by a class of quantum states
\Omega
that include the state (
107
) and satisfy (
108
) is a lattice, with the meet given by (
61
).

Proof.

For any
{\sf N}
, the top element of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is realized in quantum mechanics by the fully uncorrelated state (
107
), which by assumption belongs to
\Omega
. The set of PMIs realized by
\Omega
is in general a subset of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, which is finite, and by (the dual of)
1
it is then sufficient to show that there is a meet operation. To prove this, we use (
108
) to show that the PMI of the tensor product of two density matrices in
\Omega
is the meet (in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
) of the PMIs of the individual density matrices, i.e.,

\pi\left(\,\vec{{\sf S}}\,(\,\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\,)\,\right)=\,\pi\left(\vec{{\sf S}}(\rho)\right)\,\wedge\,\pi\left(\vec{{\sf S}}(\rho^{\prime})\right)\,,

(109)

where we keep the
{\sf N}
dependence implicit for notational clarity. To see this, notice that from the additivity of the von Neumann entropy for tensor products, namely

\vec{{\sf S}}\,(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})=\,\vec{{\sf S}}(\rho)+\vec{{\sf S}}(\rho^{\prime})\,,

(110)

we have

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})={\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho)+{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho^{\prime})\,,

(111)

where the argument of
{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})
is the quantum state on which we are evaluating the MI. This implies by SA that

{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})=0\quad\iff\quad{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho)=0\;\;\text{and}\;\;{\sf I}(\underline{\mathscr{I}}:\underline{\mathscr{K}})(\rho^{\prime})=0\,.

(112)

Therefore, the
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
for
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}
, in terms of those for
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, is given by

\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}}=\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\rho}\cap\mathcal{E}^{0}_{\rho^{\prime}}\,,

(113)

which we know from (
61
) and
2
to be the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

In light of
7
, to tackle the marginal independence problem for a class of states
\Omega
satisfying the assumptions (
107
) and (
108
), one might hope it suffices to only focus on the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
. Indeed, if all meet-irreducible elements are realizable by states in
\Omega
, then by (
109
) all other elements are also realizable.
However, the following result shows that for
{\sf N}\geq 4
this can never be the case.
55
55

55

For
{\sf N}=2,3
the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is coatomistic, and we have given explicit realizations of the coatoms by quantum states (see
Figure

4
and
Figure

5
).

Theorem 8
.

For any
{\sf N}\geq 4
, there exist
KC
-compatible
PMI
s that are not
SSA
-compatible, and therefore cannot be realized by any quantum state.

Proof.

It was shown in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
that for
{\sf N}=4
, the set of all PMIs compatible with SSA can be realized by quantum states.
56
56

56

In fact, it is sufficient to consider stabilizer states.
By
7
, it follows that this set is in fact a lattice with meet given by (
61
), and the meet-irreducible elements are the PMIs realized by the extreme rays of the cone carved by all instances of SA and SSA.
57
57

57

Notice that this does not imply that all the extreme rays of this cone can be realized by quantum states. In fact, this is not the case because of the constrained inequalities mentioned in the introduction.
Via explicit computation, it is straightforward to verify that the number of these meet-irreducible elements is strictly smaller than the number of meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{4}
. This shows that the set of
{\sf N}=4
PMIs that are SSA-compatible is properly contained in the set of
{\sf N}=4
PMIs that are KC-compatible, proving the claim for
{\sf N}=4
. The generalization to any
{\sf N}>4
is immediate using the canonical embedding described in
6
.
∎

8
implies that for any class of states
\Omega
, and any
{\sf N}\geq 4
, the lattice
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\Omega$}}^{{\sf N}}
identified by
7
is not
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. However, since the meet for both lattices is simply given by intersection, it suffices to know all the meet-irreducible elements of these lattices to construct all their elements. A useful strategy to find
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\Omega$}}^{{\sf N}}
then might be to look for its meet-irreducible elements starting from those of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
.

The proof of
8
also shows that for
{\sf N}\geq 4
there exist PMIs that are KC-compatible but not compatible with SSA. Geometrically, this means that there exist faces of the SAC
N
whose corresponding PMIs are KC-compatible, while all the vectors in their
interior
violate SSA. Thus, KC should be interpreted as an approximation of SSA, and correspondingly the KC lattice as an approximation of the lattice of SSA-compatible PMIs given by the following result.

Theorem 9
.

For any given
{\sf N}
, the set
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{SSA}}}^{{\sf N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{\text{\emph{KC}}}^{{\sf N}}
of
PMI
s which are compatible with
SSA
is a lattice, with the meet given by (
61
).

Proof.

The set inclusion
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
trivially follows from the fact that KC-compatibility is a necessary condition for SSA-compatibility for PMIs. To show that
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
is a lattice and that the meet is given by (
61
), we can proceed as in the proof of
2
. Clearly, the top element is the origin of the SAC
N
, which satisfies SSA trivially. To show that the meet is given by (
61
), notice that for any
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
, both
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
must contain at least an entropy vector
\vec{{\sf S}},\vec{{\sf S}}^{\prime}
satisfying SSA and belonging to the corresponding faces
\mathscr{F}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P})
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
of the SAC
N
. We can then consider the sum
\vec{{\sf S}}^{\prime\prime}=\vec{{\sf S}}+\vec{{\sf S}}^{\prime}
and proceed as in the proof of
7
(notice that whether
\vec{{\sf S}},\vec{{\sf S}}^{\prime}
are realizable by quantum states is immaterial). Finally, the resulting PMI is guaranteed to be SSA-compatible since by convexity
\vec{{\sf S}}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies all instances of SSA.
∎

In light of this result, a natural first step in the derivation of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{$\Omega$}}^{{\sf N}}
is then the construction of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
. Again, since the meet of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
is, like
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, also given by (
61
), one could hope to efficiently derive the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
starting from those of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
. We leave this problem, as well as the structural analysis of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
, and in particular the question of how well it approximates the KC lattice for future work, and conclude this section with a few comments about the particularly interesting case of 1-dimensional PMIs.

It was already found in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
that for all
{\sf N}\leq 5
, all extreme rays of the SAC
N
which satisfy SSA can be realized by quantum states.
58
58

58

In fact they can all be realized by stabilizer states, and they all are extreme rays of the holographic entropy cone
Bao:2015bfa
;
Cuenca:2019uzx
.
This result was obtained by the explicit computation of the extreme rays of the cone carved out by all instances of SA and SSA for
{\sf N}=5
. To explore how good KC is as an approximation of SSA for 1-dimensional PMIs, we need to find all KC-compatible extreme rays of SAC
5
. Although it is tempting to achieve this via a brute force search by simply determining all extreme rays of SAC
5
and checking which satisfy KC, the combinatorics render this search very difficult due to the doubly exponential timescales involved. This is where the power of
1
becomes apparent, since by utilizing it, we are able to easily compute the KC-compatible extreme rays for
{\sf N}=5
, leading us to make the following observation.

Observation
.

For all
{\sf N}\leq 5
, all
KC
-compatible extreme rays of the
SAC
N
satisfy
SSA
.

The same fact also holds for all currently known KC-compatible extreme rays of the highly non-trivial case of the SAC
6

He:2022wip
. Motivated by this, we pose the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1
.

For any
{\sf N}
, all
KC
-compatible extreme rays of the
SAC
N
satisfy
SSA
.

While compatible with all known data, we stress that if true, this statement would be quite a remarkable result, given that the analogous statement does not hold for all PMIs (and more generally holds only on a set of measure zero of all entropy vectors).
It would be particularly interesting to understand what property of extreme rays ensures this restricted equivalence between KC and SSA.

5
Discussion

The main goal of this work was to analyze the subset of PMIs (or equivalently faces of the SAC) which are compatible with the basic quantum mechanical fact that the mutual information between two systems vanishes only if their joint state factorizes. By elementary properties of the tensor product, this implies that the mutual information between their respective subsystems must also vanish, imposing a restriction on the set of PMIs that can be realized in quantum mechanics.
We have shown that for an arbitrary number of parties, the set of PMIs compatible with this requirement, which we dubbed Klein’s condition and conveniently rephrased using a partial order on the set of MI-instances, forms a lattice. We then explored various structural properties of this lattice, which is the content of Theorems
3
,
4
,
5
, and
6
, and proved a general result that simplifies the explicit computation of 1-dimensional KC-compatible PMIs (
1
) for an arbitrary number of parties. Lastly, we commented on the realizability of the elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
by quantum states (cf.
7
), and on the relation between KC and SSA (cf.
8
and
9
). Using
1
, we were able to find the full set of KC-compatible extreme rays of the SAC
5
and verify that it is precisely the set of extreme rays which satisfy SSA. Based on this observation, and a partial result for the case involving six parties
He:2022wip
, we conjectured the equivalence of these sets for an arbitrary number of parties (cf.
1
).

There are still many questions that remain open, both about the structure of the lattices
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{\sf N}
for arbitrary
{\sf N}
, and about their applicability to studying the set of PMIs realizable either by arbitrary quantum states, or by states in restricted classes of particular interest. Some technical questions pertaining to the structure of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
were already mentioned in §
3.4
. In this section, we would like to comment on more general open questions and future directions of investigations.

Meet-irreducible elements and closure systems:

If the join (meet) operation is known explicitly, any lattice can be constructed from its set of join-irreducible (meet-irreducible) elements. In the case of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, the join does not appear to have a simple form, as it depends on the intricate relations among the various instances of SA, which vary with
{\sf N}
. On the other hand, since PMIs are described by their
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
, the meet in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
is simply given by the
intersection of
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
(cf. (
61
)), so
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
can be easily constructed from the meet-irreducible elements. To make further progress in the analysis of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
for larger
{\sf N}
, one can then focus on these elements only.

Since (as a set)
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
is a subset of both
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
(cf. (
67
)), and the meet operation is the same for all these lattices (cf.
3
), one may attempt to derive the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
starting from those of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}
or
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
, and if necessary, take intersections of their corresponding sets of vanishing MI instances. However, in the case of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}
, the computation of the meet-irreducible elements is challenging, since it amounts to finding the extreme rays of the full SAC starting from its facets, and in general there is no efficient algorithm for such a computation. On the other hand, the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
are straightforward to derive. Since the lattice is distributive (see the paragraph above
9
), they correspond, from the point of view of the MI-poset, to the complements of the principal up-sets. An example of these elements are the PMIs of single Bell pairs, which are the complements of the principal up-sets of the minimal elements of the MI-poset (cf. proof of
5
). Notice that these Bell pairs are also meet-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}
since they are coatoms, but they are not coatoms in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
. In contrast, the complements of principal up-sets of non-minimal elements of the MI-poset do not correspond to PMIs, since they all include
\sf{D}
linearly independent MI instances,
59
59

59

This follows from the fact that these sets strictly contain the complements of the principal up-sets of minimal elements, which are coatoms of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
.
and the intersection of the corresponding hyperplanes in entropy space is the origin.

From these simple considerations one can then expect that the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
will typically be given by the meet of several meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}^{{\sf N}}
, since as
{\sf N}
grows, the width of the MI poset grows much faster than its height.
60
60

60

The width of a poset is the cardinality of its maximal antichain.
Therefore, in order to efficiently derive the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, one needs to better understand the dependence relations among the various instances of SA. A natural framework for this analysis lies in the theory of matroids
61
61

61

Matroids are combinatorial structures that abstractify the notion of linear dependence and can be axiomatized in several different ways.

oxley
(and its oriented version
MR1226888
), which provides tools to phrase the implications of the dependence among the instances of SA purely in combinatorial terms
He:2022wip
.

A matroid can also be seen as a particular instance of a more general structure called a
closure system
, i.e., a pair composed of a set
\mathcal{X}
and a
closure operator

\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
62
62

62

A closure operator
\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}
is map on the power-set of
\mathcal{X}
such that for all
\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{Y})\\
&\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\implies\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{Z})\\
&\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{Y}))=\text{cl}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{Y})\,.\end{split}

For any closure system, the set of closed subsets (with partial order given by inclusion) is a lattice. Moreover, any lattice such that its elements can be represented by sets, and its meet by intersection, is the lattice of closed sets of a closure system
davey1990introduction
. Therefore
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
,
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
can all be thought of as lattices of closed sets for different closure operators on the set
\mathcal{E}
of MI instances, and it would be interesting to explore in detail the properties of the closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
, and its relation to the closure operators of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
. The closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
is simple, as it just associates to a collection of MI instances the smallest down-set in the MI-poset that contains the collection. On the other hand, the closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
is more complicated.
63
63

63

It is associated to the closure operator that defines the oriented matroid
BUCHI1988293
of SA instances.
Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
is the intersection of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
, and given the simplicity of the closure operator for
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
, it would be interesting to understand how much information is necessary about the closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}
to extract the closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
. The general theory of lattices of closure systems, closure operators, and implicational bases
gratzer2016
;
CASPARD2003241
;
BERTET201893
provides the appropriate framework for this analysis.

Finally, we have shown in
4
that the set of join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
includes all the PMIs whose
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
correspond to the join-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
that are not principal down-sets of maximal elements in the MI-poset. Furthermore, we have also seen that for
{\sf N}\leq 4
there is no join-irreducible element in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}
whose
{\zeta\text{-down-set}}
corresponds to an element that is not join-irreducible in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{MID}}
. It would be interesting to understand whether this property holds for an arbitrary number of parties (cf.
1
), as well as its implications for the closure operator of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}

finkbeiner
.

Realizability in quantum mechanics:

We mentioned at the end of §
4
that for
{\sf N}\geq 4
there exist PMIs in
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
that are not compatible with SSA, and therefore are not realizable by quantum states. In search of the solution to the QMIP, the next natural step is then the extraction of the set of PMIs which are compatible with SSA. As shown in
9
, this set is also a lattice, with meet corresponding to the intersection of
{\zeta\text{-down-sets}}
.

A natural strategy to obtain this lattice would be to first construct a cone specified not only by the instances of SA, but also of SSA. For each face of this cone one could then determine the PMI, and the set of PMIs obtained in this fashion would give the desired lattice. However, as we mentioned in §
2.3
, this procedure quickly becomes extremely inefficient as
{\sf N}
grows, due to the fact that many faces correspond to the same PMI (cf.
Footnote

19
).

We suggest that a more promising approach instead is to utilize
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{\sf N}
as the starting point, viewing KC as an approximation of SSA for PMIs. Starting from the meet-irreducible elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
, one can first verify which elements are consistent with SSA.
64
64

64

For example, this can be done efficiently with a linear program, by first reducing all instances of SSA to the subspace of interest, and then checking for the feasibility of the resulting system of inequalities. Alternatively, it would also be interesting to obtain a purely combinatorial characterization of SSA-compatible PMIs using matroid theory.
By taking the meet of such elements, one can then determine new elements of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}
and proceed to repeat the process until one has the generating set of the desired lattice. The efficiency of this procedure will depend on how effective KC is to approximating SSA, as well as on the strategy utilized to determine which pairs of elements should be chosen to compute the meet. For instance, if one could prove
1
, it suffices then to focus only on higher dimensional meet-irreducible elements, and it would be interesting to explore whether such elements also, up to some maximal dimension independent of
{\sf N}
, all satisfy SSA automatically. More generally, it would be useful to explore these aspects of the construction in more detail, as well as the structural properties of
\mathfrak{L}_{\text{SSA}}^{{\sf N}}
.

Once the lattice of SSA-compatible PMIs has been found for some
{\sf N}
, one would like to explore if all such PMIs are realizable in quantum mechanics. As we mentioned in the introduction, for
{\sf N}\geq 4
there are no known unconstrained inequalities for the von Neumann entropy beyond SA and SSA, but infinitely many constrained inequalities have been found
Linden:2004ebt
;
Cadney_2012
. It would be an intriguing research direction to ascertain if any of these inequalities provide new restrictions on the set of PMIs which are independent from SSA. However, we already know that this does not happen for
{\sf N}=4
, since in this case all SSA-compatible PMIs are realizable by quantum states
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
.

Given an arbitrary PMI that satisfies all known entropy inequalities, it is typically hard to determine whether it can be realized by a quantum state. This is because there is no standard procedure to construct a quantum state with a desired pattern of correlation. Nevertheless, at least for the restricted class of stabilizer states, there are more tools available. One possibility is to utilize the hypergraph models of
Bao:2020zgx
, which provide direct means of constructing entropy vectors that can be realized by stabilizer states
Walter:2020zvt
.
65
65

65

The hypergraph models were introduced in
Bao:2020zgx
as a generalization of the graph models of holographic entanglement
Bao:2015bfa
, and one may wonder if the latter may also be useful for explicit realizations. However, it was shown in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
that already for
{\sf N}=4
, there exist PMIs that are realizable by stabilizer states, and in particular by hypergraph models, but not by graph models.
Even though it was shown in
Bao:2020mqq
that there are stabilizer states whose entropy vectors cannot be realized by hypergraph models, it is in principle also possible that hypergraph models are nevertheless sufficient to realize all PMIs that can be realized by stabilizer states.

Finally, it is certainly possible that there exist PMIs which can be realized by quantum states but not by stabilizer states, and presumably these would be harder to find. However, it was speculated in
Cadney_2012
that all balanced inequalities satisfied by classical probability distributions could also hold for the von Neumann entropy. As these inequalities are likewise known to be satisfied by stabilizer states
Gross_2013
, it is also interesting to contemplate the possibility that stabilizer states are in fact sufficient to realize all quantum mechanical PMIs. If this were the case, the set of PMIs realizable by quantum states would be a proper subset of the set of PMIs compatible with SSA, since already for
{\sf N}=5
there exist PMIs which are SSA-compatible but not realizable by stabilizer states
Hernandez-Cuenca:2019jpv
.

1-dimensional PMIs and applications to quantum gravity (holography):

In the previous paragraph we commented on the relation between SSA and KC, both of which are necessary conditions for the realizability of PMIs. While these conditions are in general not equivalent (cf.
8
), an open question is whether they are equivalent for the specific case of 1-dimensional PMIs. As we mentioned at the end of §
4
, we have verified this equivalence up to
{\sf N}=5
(and partially for
{\sf N}=6

He:2022wip
), and we conjecture that it holds for any
{\sf N}
(cf.
1
).

The interest in 1-dimensional PMIs stems from its potential application to the analysis of entropic constraints in the gauge/gravity duality. It was recently argued in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
that all holographic entropy inequalities might in principle be reconstructed from the knowledge of which extreme rays of the SAC can be realized by the graph models of
Bao:2015bfa
.
66
66

66

Crucially, for the reconstruction of the
{\sf N}
-party inequalities, it is in general not sufficient to know the extreme rays of the SAC
N
realized by graph models. However,
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
argued that more generally, for any
{\sf N}
, there exists a finite
{\sf N}^{\prime}\geq{\sf N}
such that this type of information would be sufficient for the reconstruction (see
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
for more details).
These observations suggest a natural question which aims at understanding the very origin of holographic entropic constraints, namely whether the extreme rays of the SAC that can be realized by graph models are precisely the ones that can be realized in quantum mechanics, or if instead there exist extreme rays that can be realized by quantum states but not by graph models.

To further comment on this question, it is useful to consider the following subsets of extreme rays of SAC
N
from
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathscr{R}&\coloneqq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{PMI}}^{{\sf N}}:\,\text{dim}(\mathbb{P})=1\}\\
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{SSA}}}&\coloneqq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{R}:\,\mathbb{P}\;\text{is SSA-compatible}\}\\
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}&\coloneqq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{R}:\,\mathbb{P}\;\text{is realizable by a quantum state}\}\\
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}&\coloneqq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{R}:\,\mathbb{P}\;\text{is realizable by a graph model}\}\,,\end{split}

(114)

and similarly define the new set

\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{KC}}}\!\coloneqq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathfrak{L}_{\text{KC}}^{{\sf N}}:\,\text{dim}(\mathbb{P})=1\}\,.

(115)

From these definitions we clearly have

\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{SSA}}}\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{KC}}}\subseteq\mathscr{R}\,,

(116)

where the last inclusion is strict for any
{\sf N}\geq 3
. While it seems reasonable to expect that
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}
could be derived explicitly using graph model constructions, determining
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}
in principle could be much harder. A tantalizing possibility suggested in
Hernandez-Cuenca:2022pst
was that
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{SSA}}}
, and the initial hope was to show it by demonstrating that in fact
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{KC}}}
. By (
116
), this would imply
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}
, which would answer the question mentioned above, as well as
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{SSA}}}
. This would provide a partial solution to the QMIP and interesting structural information about the quantum entropy cone. However, subsequently to the first version of this work, partial analysis involving
{\sf N}=6
parties already showed that
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{H}}}\subset\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}
strictly
he2023gap
. On the other hand, the possibility that
\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{Q}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{SSA}}}\!=\mathscr{R}_{{}_{\text{KC}}}
remains open, and is currently being investigated in
He:2022wip
.

Unbounded number of parties:

Finally, we observe that throughout this paper, as well as in all other previous works on entropy cones, the number of parties
{\sf N}
was always assumed to be finite. While this is natural from an operational perspective (for example, qubits in a quantum computer), in quantum field theory (QFT) there are no real restrictions to the possible refinements of a given collection of subsystems. In other words, given a state in a QFT, and
{\sf N}
regions of the manifolds on which the theory is defined, one can always imagine partitioning these regions into
{\sf N}^{\prime}>{\sf N}
smaller ones.
67
67

67

As in the case of the multi-boundary systems used in
Bao:2015bfa
, to define the holographic entropy cone we could consider a state in the tensor product of
{\sf N}
QFTs, but here we are focusing on a choice of regions that probes a single QFT.
Fixing the value of
{\sf N}
to be a specific finite number is just a matter of convenience, but this number has no real meaning and might introduce various combinatorial artifacts. For example, the structure of the MI-poset depends on the partitions of the integers between 2 and
{\sf N}
, which affects the dependence relations among the instances of SA and in turn might render the lattice more complicated. An interesting research direction would be to investigate a generalization of the analysis presented here to a set-up where
{\sf N}
is infinite, in the hope that some of these artifacts will disappear. The theory of infinite lattice might then be a natural framework for such an investigation.
